Automotive Service English

Capture customer concerns, clarify assessment approval, relay technician findings, explain parts delays, and coordinate collection and repeat-visit communication.

Words into action

For Service reception staff, service advisors, automotive technicians, parts-counter staff, and workshop assistants.

What you will practice · Choose your pace

What you will practice

  • Identify the booked vehicle and capture an intermittent customer concern without inventing its frequency, cause, or technical finding.
  • Correct an appointment misunderstanding with a specific communication promise while keeping workshop start and completion times unconfirmed.
  • Resolve ambiguous approval by reading back the exact service, charge, and limit, then separate any later repair decision.
  • Deliver a concise internal handoff that preserves the symptom, assessment-only authorization, reproduction status, and customer contact window.
  • Explain a parts delay by separating supplier estimates, shipment confirmation, workshop scheduling, and a firm update commitment.

Choose your pace

Quick practice · 15 minutes: complete one short exchange, check the explanations, and read both roles aloud.

Extended practice · two or three sittings: check the case facts, study the vocabulary and phrases, complete the longer conversation, then rehearse the follow-up exchange.

Level guide: designed for intermediate to advanced learners. B1 learners can start with the short exchanges and supplied phrases; B2 learners can complete the cloze conversations; C1 learners can focus on the distinctions in the language checks. These are teaching suggestions, not a certified level assessment.

Your eight lessons

01 Capturing the customer's concern02 Clarifying the appointment and drop-off03 Explaining estimates and approval limits04 Handing the concern to the technician05 Explaining parts and completion delays06 Relaying findings and technical terms07 Clarifying the invoice and collection handoff08 Handling a repeat concern and follow-up
Prompts

The vehicles, shop terms, charges, records, and events are fictional. This book teaches English, not vehicle diagnosis, repair, testing, driving decisions, or legal advice. Follow actual training, authorization, manufacturer information, shop procedures, and applicable requirements. A code, invoice, or customer description does not establish vehicle safety, readiness, repair necessity, or warranty coverage. The dialogues give no instructions to reproduce symptoms while driving or work on a vehicle.

01Capturing the customer's concernThe blue hatchback and an occasional rear rattle+

Your goal

Identify the booked vehicle and capture an intermittent customer concern without inventing its frequency, cause, or technical finding.

This lesson in the book, page 4 ↗

A Read the situation

The blue hatchback and an occasional rear rattle

Two household cars are listed on the account, but today Mina has booked the blue hatchback. She reports an occasional rattle from the rear after the car has been parked overnight. She does not know the cause, and no technician assessment has occurred. Advisor Ellis must confirm the vehicle, preserve the reported sound and conditions, and read back the concern. Mina is describing existing observations; she is not asked to drive or perform a test to reproduce the sound.

Mina: Customer / Ellis: Service advisor

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which vehicle is booked today?

Answer and explanation

D. The blue hatchback

The brief identifies the blue hatchback as the vehicle for this booking.

2. How does Mina describe the sound?

Answer and explanation

B. An occasional rattle from the rear

The report is intermittent and located toward the rear, without a confirmed cause.

3. What assessment has occurred?

Answer and explanation

C. None by a technician yet

No technician assessment has taken place at the point of this conversation.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The booked vehicle is the blue hatchback, not both cars on the account. The sound is occasional and reported from the rear after overnight parking. Do not change those details to a constant noise or a named failed component.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
service advisor
Staff member coordinating customer communication and workshop work.brief the service advisor
customer concern
Issue described by the vehicle owner or user.capture the customer concern
vehicle identification
Information establishing which vehicle a record concerns.confirm vehicle identification
service interval
Specified time or accumulated-distance basis for scheduled maintenance, subject to the actual vehicle schedule.check the service interval
hatchback
Car body style with an upward-opening rear door to the cargo area.identify the blue hatchback
booking reference
Identifier for a scheduled service appointment.confirm the booking reference
tire aspect ratio
Nominal sidewall height expressed as a percentage of nominal section width in a tire-size designation.read the tire aspect ratio
vehicle identification number
Unique vehicle identifier commonly abbreviated VIN.confirm the vehicle identification number
load index
Tire rating code associated with load capacity, not a literal weight in kilograms.verify the load index
model
Named vehicle design within a manufacturer's range.confirm the model
state of charge
Battery charge level expressed as a percentage on the applicable battery system's basis.distinguish state of charge from capacity
odometer
Instrument recording accumulated vehicle distance.record the odometer reading
intermittent
Occurring at intervals rather than continuously.describe an intermittent concern
rattle
Repeated light knocking or vibrating sound.describe the reported rattle
rear
Part or direction toward the back of the vehicle.locate the sound at the rear
overnight parking
Period during which a vehicle remains parked through the night.note overnight parking
reported conditions
Circumstances described as accompanying the concern.preserve the reported conditions
frequency
How often a symptom is reported to occur.clarify the reported frequency
total toe
Combined toe value for the two wheels on an axle under the report's sign convention.read total toe
symptom description
Account of the experienced sound, behavior, or condition.retain the symptom description
technician assessment
Relevant professional evaluation of the concern.distinguish a technician assessment
angular minute
One sixtieth of a degree, not one hundredth of a degree.convert angular minutes to decimal degrees
job card
Workshop record containing the requested work and relevant details.prepare the job card
concern readback
Repetition of the customer's account to confirm accuracy.give a concern readback

No technician assessment has occurred. The readback should preserve what Mina has already experienced without asking her to reproduce a symptom while driving or inventing details about speed, exact duration, cause, or vehicle safety.

C Notice the language

Capture the description before translating it into workshop shorthand

A customer can describe a sound accurately without knowing a component name. Confirm the vehicle and repeat the reported conditions. Keep everyday wording when it carries useful meaning, and avoid leading questions that make a suspected part sound like an established fault.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Confirm the vehicle
Which of the two cars is booked today?
Repeat the selection
I have the blue hatchback for this appointment.
Keep the account separate
I will not attach this concern to the other vehicle.
Ask for the existing description
How have you described the sound you noticed?
Preserve the sound
You report a rattle from the rear.
Preserve frequency
It is occasional, not constant.
Clarify the condition
You have noticed it after the car was parked overnight.
Avoid inventing a cause
You have not identified what causes it.
Keep assessment separate
No technician assessment has taken place yet.
Avoid leading terminology
I will record rattle without naming a failed component.
Keep the record attributed
The customer reports the following concern.
Avoid a false guarantee
This description does not establish whether the vehicle is fault-free.
Distinguish identity fields
A model name and a vehicle identification number are different.
Ask for confirmation
Does that wording accurately describe your experience?
Give the complete readback
Blue hatchback; occasional rear rattle after overnight parking; cause unknown.
Close with the handoff
I will put that concern on the job card for assessment.
6 language and precision notes
Occasional versus constant
Preserves how often the sound is reported without inventing a frequency.
From the rear
Locates the perceived sound without identifying a particular component.
After overnight parking
Records a circumstance, not necessarily a proven cause.
Customer reports
Separates the account from a technician finding.
Has not taken place yet
Identifies the current assessment stage.
Does that wording ...?
Invites a bounded accuracy check instead of a request to diagnose the vehicle.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which job-card wording preserves the account?

Answer and explanation

A. Customer reports occasional rear rattle after overnight parking in blue hatchback

The wording retains vehicle, attribution, sound, frequency, and reported conditions.

2. Which question is leading?

Answer and explanation

B. Which rear component has failed?

The question assumes a failed component before any assessment has occurred.

3. What should Ellis not add?

Answer and explanation

D. A precise cause or unreported driving condition

The account contains no verified cause or additional driving conditions.

4. Why should the vehicle be confirmed first?

Answer and explanation

B. Two cars are on the account and only one is booked

Confirming identity prevents the concern from being attached to the wrong household vehicle.

Check explanations in the book, page 85 ↗

E Conversations

The blue hatchback and an occasional rear rattle

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Mina: Customer / Ellis: Service advisor

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • reported conditions
  • symptom description
  • frequency
  • technician assessment
  • intermittent
  • job card
  • customer concern
  • concern readback
  • vehicle identification
  • booking reference
  1. Mina
    I've booked the blue hatchback for a rattle. There are two cars on our account; could you check which one you've opened?
  2. Ellis
    Certainly. Let us confirm the 1 first. Which of the two cars is booked for today's appointment?
  3. Mina
    Yes, the blue hatchback. Today's concern isn't about the other car.
  4. Ellis
    I will keep the 2 with the blue hatchback and make sure the concern is recorded there. How would you describe the sound you have noticed?
  5. Mina
    It's an occasional rattle from the rear. I've noticed it after the car's been parked overnight.
  6. Ellis
    I will record an 3 rattle from the rear after overnight parking. In this context, intermittent means occasional rather than continuous; is that consistent with your experience?
  7. Mina
    Occasional is right. I can't tell you how many times, and I wouldn't say it happens every morning.
  8. Ellis
    Then we will preserve the reported 4 as occasional, without inventing a count. The technician should receive what you know, not a more definite pattern than you described.
  9. Mina
    Rear is as specific as I can be. I don't know which part is making it.
  10. Ellis
    That is enough for the 5. We can keep rear as the reported location without turning it into a claim that a particular component has failed.
  11. Mina
    Nobody's assessed it yet. I don't want my guess written down as a diagnosis.
  12. Ellis
    I will make that stage clear. No 6 has taken place, and the cause is unknown. The record will not present your description as a workshop finding.
  13. Mina
    Please keep after overnight parking in the note. I don't know whether that's the cause, but it's when I've noticed it.
  14. Ellis
    Agreed. Those are 7, not an established explanation. I will not add a speed, journey length, or other circumstance that you have not supplied.
  15. Mina
    Will those details reach the technician? I don't want the job card to end up saying only noisy car.
  16. Ellis
    That helps us produce a useful 8. Ordinary words such as rattle can carry important detail when the vehicle, location, and circumstances are accurately recorded.
  17. Mina
    Yes, that readback matches. No fixed frequency, and no part identified.
  18. Ellis
    Here is the 9: customer reports an occasional rear rattle in the blue hatchback after overnight parking. Cause unknown; no technician assessment has occurred.
  19. Mina
    Thank you. That's what I wanted recorded, without making the sound seem constant.
  20. Ellis
    I will retain that 10 for the workshop handoff. The next assessment can build on an accurate description instead of starting from an assumed cause.

Answers and explanations
  1. vehicle identification Turn 2. Vehicle identification prevents this concern from being recorded against the other household car.
  2. booking reference Turn 4. Booking reference connects today's appointment to the selected vehicle rather than both cars.
  3. intermittent Turn 6. Intermittent preserves Mina's description of an occasional sound rather than a constant one.
  4. frequency Turn 8. Frequency is limited to occasional because Mina has not supplied a count or fixed pattern.
  5. symptom description Turn 10. Symptom description captures the experienced sound and location without requiring a component diagnosis.
  6. technician assessment Turn 12. Technician assessment has not occurred, so the concern must remain attributed to the customer.
  7. reported conditions Turn 14. Reported conditions preserve the overnight-parking circumstance without treating it as proven causation.
  8. job card Turn 16. Job card is the workshop record that should preserve the precise customer concern for assessment.
  9. concern readback Turn 18. Concern readback checks the vehicle, intermittent sound, reported conditions, and unknown cause together.
  10. customer concern Turn 20. Customer concern remains the owner's account, distinct from a later diagnosis or vehicle-safety conclusion.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Mina: I've booked the blue hatchback for a rattle. There are two cars on our account; could you check which one you've opened?
  2. Ellis: Certainly. Let us confirm the vehicle identification first. Which of the two cars is booked for today's appointment?
  3. Mina: Yes, the blue hatchback. Today's concern isn't about the other car.
  4. Ellis: I will keep the booking reference with the blue hatchback and make sure the concern is recorded there. How would you describe the sound you have noticed?
  5. Mina: It's an occasional rattle from the rear. I've noticed it after the car's been parked overnight.
  6. Ellis: I will record an intermittent rattle from the rear after overnight parking. In this context, intermittent means occasional rather than continuous; is that consistent with your experience?
  7. Mina: Occasional is right. I can't tell you how many times, and I wouldn't say it happens every morning.
  8. Ellis: Then we will preserve the reported frequency as occasional, without inventing a count. The technician should receive what you know, not a more definite pattern than you described.
  9. Mina: Rear is as specific as I can be. I don't know which part is making it.
  10. Ellis: That is enough for the symptom description. We can keep rear as the reported location without turning it into a claim that a particular component has failed.
  11. Mina: Nobody's assessed it yet. I don't want my guess written down as a diagnosis.
  12. Ellis: I will make that stage clear. No technician assessment has taken place, and the cause is unknown. The record will not present your description as a workshop finding.
  13. Mina: Please keep after overnight parking in the note. I don't know whether that's the cause, but it's when I've noticed it.
  14. Ellis: Agreed. Those are reported conditions, not an established explanation. I will not add a speed, journey length, or other circumstance that you have not supplied.
  15. Mina: Will those details reach the technician? I don't want the job card to end up saying only noisy car.
  16. Ellis: That helps us produce a useful job card. Ordinary words such as rattle can carry important detail when the vehicle, location, and circumstances are accurately recorded.
  17. Mina: Yes, that readback matches. No fixed frequency, and no part identified.
  18. Ellis: Here is the concern readback: customer reports an occasional rear rattle in the blue hatchback after overnight parking. Cause unknown; no technician assessment has occurred.
  19. Mina: Thank you. That's what I wanted recorded, without making the sound seem constant.
  20. Ellis: I will retain that customer concern for the workshop handoff. The next assessment can build on an accurate description instead of starting from an assumed cause.
Dialogue in the book, page 10 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Two cars, one reported rattle

At reception, an advisor clarifies the customer's vehicle and concern.

  1. Advisor: Which car is today's concern about, the blue hatchback or your white sedan?
  2. Customer: The blue hatchback. I sometimes hear a rattle from the rear after overnight parking.
  3. Advisor: Does it happen every morning, or only occasionally when you first notice it?
  4. Customer: Only occasionally. I can't identify a component, and I haven't noticed a consistent pattern.
  5. Advisor: I'll record an intermittent concern, with your rear-rattle description and the overnight-parking detail.
  6. Customer: Please don't call it a suspension fault. That was just someone's suggestion online.
  7. Advisor: I won't. We need the technician's assessment before naming a cause on the repair order.
  8. Customer: Can you make sure the account's other car doesn't inherit this concern too?
  9. Advisor: Yes. We'll verify the vehicle identification number against this booking and vehicle record.
  10. Customer: Thank you. That keeps my symptom description attached to the car actually being assessed.
The sound word needs clarification

An advisor asks a customer to clarify an informal noise description.

  1. Customer: I wrote clicking on the booking, but rattling might describe the sound better.
  2. Advisor: Could you describe what you noticed without worrying about choosing a technical word?
  3. Customer: It was several quick little sounds, not one loud impact, near the passenger side.
  4. Advisor: I'll preserve that description rather than translate it into a component diagnosis.
  5. Customer: Please say it was my impression of the location, not something I verified.
  6. Advisor: Certainly. I'll mark passenger side as reported, with the exact source unconfirmed.
  7. Customer: Does changing my word from clicking to rattling mean the booking needs canceling?
  8. Advisor: No. We can clarify the concern on the existing repair order for this vehicle.
  9. Customer: Then include both my earlier wording and the clearer description we're discussing now.
  10. Advisor: I will. The technician can see how your description was clarified at intake.
Yesterday is not every day

An advisor checks the timing of a customer's concern.

  1. Advisor: The booking says the display goes blank daily. Is that frequency accurate?
  2. Customer: No. It happened yesterday once; I don't know why I selected daily online.
  3. Advisor: Thanks for correcting it. Had you noticed the same display issue before yesterday?
  4. Customer: Not that I remember. I don't want to claim it has never happened.
  5. Advisor: I'll record one episode reported yesterday, with no earlier episode recalled by you.
  6. Customer: That sounds right. Can the technician see that I corrected the original frequency?
  7. Advisor: Yes. I'll clarify the intake record so daily isn't treated as confirmed history.
  8. Customer: Please also distinguish the display from the whole electrical system. I can't assess that.
  9. Advisor: Agreed. Your report concerns the display only, and the cause remains unconfirmed.
  10. Customer: Then the symptom description now reflects what I actually observed rather than assumptions.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

The blue hatchback and an occasional rear rattle
  1. Read the concern in the brief, then complete the dialogue using the supplied word bank.
  2. Read Mina's turns aloud, stressing occasional, rear, and after overnight parking. Then read Ellis's matching readback.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Read back the booked concern

Complete the intake summary. Preserve the correct car, sound, location, and reported condition.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Advisor: "The booked vehicle is the blue ___."

Answer and explanation

D. hatchback

Hatchback identifies the selected household car for today's booking.

2. Customer: "I report an occasional ___."

Answer and explanation

B. rattle

Rattle is the sound Mina described, not a diagnosis.

3. Advisor: "You hear it from the ___."

Answer and explanation

A. rear

Rear preserves the perceived location without naming a failed component.

4. Customer: "I have noticed it after ___ parking."

Answer and explanation

C. overnight

Overnight is the reported circumstance, not a proven cause of the noise.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

These prompts are fully written and include this lesson's facts and language. Open one, copy it as written, and paste it into a new AI chat.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Lesson 01: Capturing the customer's concern
Original fictional case: Two household cars are listed on the account, but today Mina has booked the blue hatchback. She reports an occasional rattle from the rear after the car has been parked overnight. She does not know the cause, and no technician assessment has occurred. Advisor Ellis must confirm the vehicle, preserve the reported sound and conditions, and read back the concern. Mina is describing existing observations; she is not asked to drive or perform a test to reproduce the sound.
Language workshop: The blue hatchback and an occasional rear rattle
Communication goal: Identify the booked vehicle and capture an intermittent customer concern without inventing its frequency, cause, or technical finding.
Language guidance: The booked vehicle is the blue hatchback, not both cars on the account. The sound is occasional and reported from the rear after overnight parking. Do not change those details to a constant noise or a named failed component.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Which of the two cars is booked today? / I have the blue hatchback for this appointment. / I will not attach this concern to the other vehicle. / How have you described the sound you noticed? / You report a rattle from the rear. / It is occasional, not constant. / You have noticed it after the car was parked overnight. / You have not identified what causes it. / No technician assessment has taken place yet. / I will record rattle without naming a failed component. / The customer reports the following concern. / This description does not establish whether the vehicle is fault-free. / A model name and a vehicle identification number are different. / Does that wording accurately describe your experience? / Blue hatchback; occasional rear rattle after overnight parking; cause unknown. / I will put that concern on the job card for assessment.
Vocabulary: service advisor: Staff member coordinating customer communication and workshop work. / customer concern: Issue described by the vehicle owner or user. / vehicle identification: Information establishing which vehicle a record concerns. / service interval: Specified time or accumulated-distance basis for scheduled maintenance, subject to the actual vehicle schedule. / hatchback: Car body style with an upward-opening rear door to the cargo area. / booking reference: Identifier for a scheduled service appointment. / tire aspect ratio: Nominal sidewall height expressed as a percentage of nominal section width in a tire-size designation. / vehicle identification number: Unique vehicle identifier commonly abbreviated VIN. / load index: Tire rating code associated with load capacity, not a literal weight in kilograms. / model: Named vehicle design within a manufacturer's range. / state of charge: Battery charge level expressed as a percentage on the applicable battery system's basis. / odometer: Instrument recording accumulated vehicle distance. / intermittent: Occurring at intervals rather than continuously. / rattle: Repeated light knocking or vibrating sound. / rear: Part or direction toward the back of the vehicle. / overnight parking: Period during which a vehicle remains parked through the night. / reported conditions: Circumstances described as accompanying the concern. / frequency: How often a symptom is reported to occur. / total toe: Combined toe value for the two wheels on an axle under the report's sign convention. / symptom description: Account of the experienced sound, behavior, or condition. / technician assessment: Relevant professional evaluation of the concern. / angular minute: One sixtieth of a degree, not one hundredth of a degree. / job card: Workshop record containing the requested work and relevant details. / concern readback: Repetition of the customer's account to confirm accuracy.
Speaking task: Read the concern in the brief, then complete the dialogue using the supplied word bank. Read Mina's turns aloud, stressing occasional, rear, and after overnight parking. Then read Ellis's matching readback. Complete the four-line transfer and check each answer against the explained key. Keep the vehicle and reported conditions unchanged.
Partner: Mina: Customer / Ellis: Service advisor
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the intake summary. Preserve the correct car, sound, location, and reported condition.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Mina: Yes, that readback matches. No fixed frequency, and no part identified.
Ellis: Here is the concern readback: customer reports an occasional rear rattle in the blue hatchback after overnight parking. Cause unknown; no technician assessment has occurred.
Mina: Thank you. That's what I wanted recorded, without making the sound seem constant.
Ellis: I will retain that customer concern for the workshop handoff. The next assessment can build on an accurate description instead of starting from an assumed cause.

Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Lesson 01: Capturing the customer's concern
Original fictional case: Two household cars are listed on the account, but today Mina has booked the blue hatchback. She reports an occasional rattle from the rear after the car has been parked overnight. She does not know the cause, and no technician assessment has occurred. Advisor Ellis must confirm the vehicle, preserve the reported sound and conditions, and read back the concern. Mina is describing existing observations; she is not asked to drive or perform a test to reproduce the sound.
Language workshop: The blue hatchback and an occasional rear rattle
Communication goal: Identify the booked vehicle and capture an intermittent customer concern without inventing its frequency, cause, or technical finding.
Language guidance: The booked vehicle is the blue hatchback, not both cars on the account. The sound is occasional and reported from the rear after overnight parking. Do not change those details to a constant noise or a named failed component.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Which of the two cars is booked today? / I have the blue hatchback for this appointment. / I will not attach this concern to the other vehicle. / How have you described the sound you noticed? / You report a rattle from the rear. / It is occasional, not constant. / You have noticed it after the car was parked overnight. / You have not identified what causes it. / No technician assessment has taken place yet. / I will record rattle without naming a failed component. / The customer reports the following concern. / This description does not establish whether the vehicle is fault-free. / A model name and a vehicle identification number are different. / Does that wording accurately describe your experience? / Blue hatchback; occasional rear rattle after overnight parking; cause unknown. / I will put that concern on the job card for assessment.
Vocabulary: service advisor: Staff member coordinating customer communication and workshop work. / customer concern: Issue described by the vehicle owner or user. / vehicle identification: Information establishing which vehicle a record concerns. / service interval: Specified time or accumulated-distance basis for scheduled maintenance, subject to the actual vehicle schedule. / hatchback: Car body style with an upward-opening rear door to the cargo area. / booking reference: Identifier for a scheduled service appointment. / tire aspect ratio: Nominal sidewall height expressed as a percentage of nominal section width in a tire-size designation. / vehicle identification number: Unique vehicle identifier commonly abbreviated VIN. / load index: Tire rating code associated with load capacity, not a literal weight in kilograms. / model: Named vehicle design within a manufacturer's range. / state of charge: Battery charge level expressed as a percentage on the applicable battery system's basis. / odometer: Instrument recording accumulated vehicle distance. / intermittent: Occurring at intervals rather than continuously. / rattle: Repeated light knocking or vibrating sound. / rear: Part or direction toward the back of the vehicle. / overnight parking: Period during which a vehicle remains parked through the night. / reported conditions: Circumstances described as accompanying the concern. / frequency: How often a symptom is reported to occur. / total toe: Combined toe value for the two wheels on an axle under the report's sign convention. / symptom description: Account of the experienced sound, behavior, or condition. / technician assessment: Relevant professional evaluation of the concern. / angular minute: One sixtieth of a degree, not one hundredth of a degree. / job card: Workshop record containing the requested work and relevant details. / concern readback: Repetition of the customer's account to confirm accuracy.
Speaking task: Read the concern in the brief, then complete the dialogue using the supplied word bank. Read Mina's turns aloud, stressing occasional, rear, and after overnight parking. Then read Ellis's matching readback. Complete the four-line transfer and check each answer against the explained key. Keep the vehicle and reported conditions unchanged.
Partner: Mina: Customer / Ellis: Service advisor
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the intake summary. Preserve the correct car, sound, location, and reported condition.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Mina: Yes, that readback matches. No fixed frequency, and no part identified.
Ellis: Here is the concern readback: customer reports an occasional rear rattle in the blue hatchback after overnight parking. Cause unknown; no technician assessment has occurred.
Mina: Thank you. That's what I wanted recorded, without making the sound seem constant.
Ellis: I will retain that customer concern for the workshop handoff. The next assessment can build on an accurate description instead of starting from an assumed cause.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

02Clarifying the appointment and drop-offEight is intake, ten is an update+

Your goal

Correct an appointment misunderstanding with a specific communication promise while keeping workshop start and completion times unconfirmed.

This lesson in the book, page 13 ↗

A Read the situation

Eight is intake, ten is an update

Customer Omar has booked an 08:00 drop-off and expects work to start immediately and finish by 09:00. The booking confirms intake only. Advisor Leila can offer a status update by 10:00, not a completion guarantee. Omar prefers a phone call. Leila must explain the distinction, acknowledge the timing expectation, and record the call preference. No technician start slot, repair duration, or collection time has been confirmed.

Omar: Customer / Leila: Service advisor

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does 08:00 confirm?

Answer and explanation

C. Intake at drop-off

The booking confirms drop-off intake, not a workshop start or completion time.

2. What can Leila promise?

Answer and explanation

C. A status update by 10:00

The supplied commitment is an update deadline rather than a finished vehicle.

3. How does Omar prefer to receive the update?

Answer and explanation

B. By phone call

The brief explicitly identifies a phone call as the preferred method.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

An 08:00 drop-off is not a confirmed 08:00 technician start. Nothing establishes completion by 09:00. Preserve the booked intake while correcting the unsupported assumptions about work and turnaround.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
drop-off
Delivery of a vehicle to the service location.confirm the drop-off time
intake
Initial process of receiving the vehicle and recording the request.distinguish intake from workshop work
appointment
Arranged time for the stated service interaction.clarify what the appointment covers
workshop start
Point when the assigned technical work begins.confirm the workshop start
technician slot
Scheduled period assigned to a technician for work.verify the technician slot
completion time
Time when the defined work is expected or confirmed to finish.distinguish completion time
collection time
Time agreed for the customer to receive the vehicle.confirm the collection time
status update
Communication reporting the current position of the work.provide a status update
update deadline
Latest time promised for further information.meet the update deadline
phone preference
Customer choice to receive communication by telephone.record the phone preference
contact method
Means used to communicate with the customer.confirm the contact method
callback number
Number agreed for a return telephone call.verify the callback number
booking terms
Stated conditions defining what an appointment includes.clarify the booking terms
timing expectation
Customer understanding of when something will happen.acknowledge the timing expectation
turnaround
Time between receiving work and returning the completed item.avoid promising an unverified turnaround
work queue
Ordered or managed set of jobs awaiting attention.distinguish the work queue
progress report
Account of how far the work has advanced.give a progress report
estimated finish
Provisional prediction of completion time.qualify the estimated finish
confirmed finish
Completion time explicitly established through the relevant process.distinguish a confirmed finish
waiting appointment
Booking specifically arranged for a customer to wait under stated terms.verify a waiting appointment
transport arrangement
Plan for the customer's travel while the vehicle is at the shop.discuss a transport arrangement
availability window
Period when a person can be contacted or attend.record the availability window
schedule clarification
Correction of an ambiguous or mistaken timing understanding.give a schedule clarification
commitment readback
Repetition of agreed promises and their limits.give a commitment readback

By 10:00 is an update deadline, not a vehicle-ready time. The promised contact is a phone call. Do not relabel the appointment as a waiting service or invent collection, transport, or workshop arrangements.

C Notice the language

Replace a mistaken promise with a real one

A customer who hears appointment may imagine a dedicated work slot. Explain the actual booking without blaming the customer. A specific update time and preferred contact method create a useful commitment while leaving unconfirmed completion information honest.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the expectation
I understand that you expected the work to start at eight.
Clarify the booking
The 08:00 booking is for drop-off intake.
Separate the work slot
It does not confirm an 08:00 technician start.
Avoid a one-hour promise
Completion by 09:00 has not been confirmed.
Offer a real commitment
I can provide a status update by 10:00.
Name the communication
That is an update, not a finish guarantee.
Confirm the preference
You would prefer a phone call.
Record the method
I will keep phone as the contact method.
Keep collection separate
We still need actual confirmation before giving a collection time.
Avoid inventing a waiting booking
This record does not confirm a waiting appointment.
Use the deadline accurately
By ten means no later than ten for the update.
Preserve the intake time
Your drop-off booking remains at 08:00.
Avoid assuming transport
We have not agreed a separate transport arrangement here.
Give an honest limit
I cannot promise a work duration that has not been established.
Read back the commitments
Drop-off at eight; phone update by ten; finish time unconfirmed.
Close with clarity
I will keep those three points separate in the booking notes.
6 language and precision notes
For drop-off
Defines the event covered by the appointment.
Does not confirm
Limits the inference without canceling the actual booking.
By versus at
By gives a latest time; at identifies a particular time.
Update, not finish
Prevents a communication promise from becoming a completion guarantee.
Would prefer
Records the customer's chosen communication method.
Remains
Preserves the valid booking while correcting its misinterpretation.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which statement best corrects Omar's expectation?

Answer and explanation

C. Eight is drop-off intake; start and finish are not confirmed

The correction explains the actual appointment while preserving uncertainty about workshop timing.

2. What does by 10:00 mean here?

Answer and explanation

B. The status call will be provided no later than 10:00

By establishes the latest promised communication time, not completion.

3. Which detail belongs in the contact notes?

Answer and explanation

B. Phone call preferred

The notes should preserve the explicitly stated phone preference.

4. Which promise exceeds Leila's stated authority?

Answer and explanation

C. The car will definitely be ready by 09:00

No completion guarantee is supplied, so a nine o'clock promise is unsupported.

Check explanations in the book, page 87 ↗

E Conversations

Eight is intake, ten is an update

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Omar: Customer / Leila: Service advisor

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • status update
  • completion time
  • waiting appointment
  • commitment readback
  • phone preference
  • intake
  • drop-off
  • contact method
  • update deadline
  • collection time
  1. Omar
    I'm dropping it off at eight. Can I wait and collect it at nine? I thought eight was when the technician would start.
  2. Leila
    I understand. I need to clarify that the booking is for 1 at 08:00. It does not confirm an eight o'clock technician start or a nine o'clock finish.
  3. Omar
    I see. I had treated the drop-off time as the start of the work.
  4. Leila
    Eight is the 2 time for receiving the vehicle and the service request. The technical start and duration have not been confirmed in this booking.
  5. Omar
    Then nine isn't confirmed either. I need to know that before I arrange the rest of my morning.
  6. Leila
    That is important. We do not have a confirmed 3, so I should not repeat your nine o'clock expectation as though it were a promise from the workshop.
  7. Omar
    What can you commit to today, even if you can't give me a collection time yet?
  8. Leila
    I can give you a 4 by 10:00. That will report the position at that stage; it is not a guarantee that the vehicle will be ready then.
  9. Omar
    By ten, rather than at ten exactly? I don't want to miss the call because I've assumed a fixed time.
  10. Leila
    I will record that 5. The promised update will be by phone, using the agreed contact details in your booking.
  11. Omar
    Phone is best. Please keep that with the update deadline.
  12. Leila
    Yes. Ten is the 6, not a fixed completion appointment. Keeping those separate should make the booking notes and your expectations consistent.
  13. Omar
    So I shouldn't tell someone I'm booked to wait here while it's done.
  14. Leila
    Correct. A 7 is not confirmed here. We should not add that assumption merely because you initially hoped to stay for an hour.
  15. Omar
    And nothing has been agreed about a lift or other transport. I'll keep that separate.
  16. Leila
    Exactly. The 8 remains unconfirmed. A communication promise helps you plan the next decision, but it does not itself establish that the vehicle can be handed back.
  17. Omar
    Could you read back what is actually booked, without the nine o'clock assumption?
  18. Leila
    I will keep the 9 alongside the deadline. The handoff should say phone update by 10:00, not simply contact customer when convenient.
  19. Omar
    Understood: drop-off at eight, phone update by ten, no confirmed finish or collection time.
  20. Leila
    That 10 is accurate. I will record it that way so the next colleague sees what is booked, what is promised, and what still needs confirmation.

Answers and explanations
  1. drop-off Turn 2. Drop-off identifies delivery of the vehicle, not an immediate start of technical work.
  2. intake Turn 4. Intake is the booked process of receiving the vehicle and recording the request.
  3. completion time Turn 6. Completion time has not been established by the intake booking or the customer's expectation.
  4. status update Turn 8. Status update is the communication Leila can promise, distinct from completed work.
  5. phone preference Turn 10. Phone preference preserves Omar's explicit choice of a telephone call rather than email.
  6. update deadline Turn 12. Update deadline is the latest promised time for the call, not an exact repair completion time.
  7. waiting appointment Turn 14. Waiting appointment would need its own stated arrangement and cannot be inferred from this intake booking.
  8. collection time Turn 16. Collection time needs actual readiness confirmation and is not established by the update deadline.
  9. contact method Turn 18. Contact method records how the promised update should reach Omar.
  10. commitment readback Turn 20. Commitment readback preserves the actual intake and call promises without inventing workshop timing.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Omar: I'm dropping it off at eight. Can I wait and collect it at nine? I thought eight was when the technician would start.
  2. Leila: I understand. I need to clarify that the booking is for drop-off at 08:00. It does not confirm an eight o'clock technician start or a nine o'clock finish.
  3. Omar: I see. I had treated the drop-off time as the start of the work.
  4. Leila: Eight is the intake time for receiving the vehicle and the service request. The technical start and duration have not been confirmed in this booking.
  5. Omar: Then nine isn't confirmed either. I need to know that before I arrange the rest of my morning.
  6. Leila: That is important. We do not have a confirmed completion time, so I should not repeat your nine o'clock expectation as though it were a promise from the workshop.
  7. Omar: What can you commit to today, even if you can't give me a collection time yet?
  8. Leila: I can give you a status update by 10:00. That will report the position at that stage; it is not a guarantee that the vehicle will be ready then.
  9. Omar: By ten, rather than at ten exactly? I don't want to miss the call because I've assumed a fixed time.
  10. Leila: I will record that phone preference. The promised update will be by phone, using the agreed contact details in your booking.
  11. Omar: Phone is best. Please keep that with the update deadline.
  12. Leila: Yes. Ten is the update deadline, not a fixed completion appointment. Keeping those separate should make the booking notes and your expectations consistent.
  13. Omar: So I shouldn't tell someone I'm booked to wait here while it's done.
  14. Leila: Correct. A waiting appointment is not confirmed here. We should not add that assumption merely because you initially hoped to stay for an hour.
  15. Omar: And nothing has been agreed about a lift or other transport. I'll keep that separate.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Eight is the drop-off time

An advisor clarifies an 08:00 booking before the customer leaves.

  1. Customer: My appointment is eight, so I expected the car finished by nine today.
  2. Advisor: Eight is your drop-off intake, not a confirmed technician start or completion time.
  3. Customer: I misunderstood. I booked a ride based on having the car back within an hour.
  4. Advisor: I'm sorry the timing wasn't clear. We can provide a phone update by ten.
  5. Customer: Will that call tell me the assessment status, or guarantee the work is finished?
  6. Advisor: It will give the actual status. It isn't a promise of completion by ten.
  7. Customer: Please call rather than text. I can answer at the number on my booking.
  8. Advisor: I've confirmed that contact preference. Is the revised timing clear before you decide?
  9. Customer: Yes. I understand the drop-off appointment and the callback, but no finish time is guaranteed.
  10. Advisor: That's the arrangement. We'll keep any later completion estimate separate from the intake time.
Waiting does not settle the finish time

A customer asks reception about remaining at the shop.

  1. Customer: I selected a waiting appointment. Does that mean you'll finish before my eleven-o'clock meeting?
  2. Receptionist: The booking records your plan to wait, but no eleven-o'clock completion is confirmed.
  3. Customer: I need to know that now rather than discover it after the intake check.
  4. Receptionist: Of course. I can ask the service advisor to review timing before you proceed.
  5. Customer: Please tell them the meeting is fixed, but I can choose another appointment.
  6. Receptionist: I'll give them that constraint and request available alternatives, not cancel this booking automatically.
  7. Customer: Could the advisor explain whether leaving the vehicle would change the timing estimate?
  8. Receptionist: Yes. That is a scheduling question for them; I can't guarantee a faster result.
  9. Customer: Then I'll wait for that explanation before deciding whether to keep today's appointment.
  10. Receptionist: I'll connect you now, with the current booking and your meeting time clearly stated.
A text cannot reach the customer

An advisor verifies a callback arrangement at drop-off.

  1. Advisor: Your booking lists text messages as the preferred contact method for today's updates.
  2. Customer: That number belongs to an old phone. Please use a call to my current number.
  3. Advisor: Let's confirm the current number through our booking check before changing the contact record.
  4. Customer: Certainly. I also can't answer between one and two because I'm presenting at work.
  5. Advisor: I'll record that availability limit alongside your preference for a phone call today.
  6. Customer: Does giving a new number authorize additional work if someone reaches me there?
  7. Advisor: No. Contact details only arrange communication; any work decision needs its own clear confirmation.
  8. Customer: Thank you. Please make that distinction if another advisor takes over this afternoon.
  9. Advisor: I'll include the verified number, call preference, and unavailable period in the handoff.
  10. Customer: Then the next advisor can reach me without assuming my contact update approves repairs.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Eight is intake, ten is an update
  1. Complete the dialogue, distinguishing drop-off, workshop start, update, and collection.
  2. Read the time exchanges aloud: eight for drop-off, by ten for the phone update, nine not confirmed. Switch roles and repeat.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Answer and explanation

2. Customer: "I prefer a ___ call."

Answer and explanation

C. phone

Phone is the contact method explicitly requested by the customer.

3. Advisor: "The status update will be provided by ___."

Answer and explanation

B. 10:00

Ten is the latest promised update time, not the completion deadline.

4. Customer: "The finish time remains ___."

Original fictional case: Customer Omar has booked an 08:00 drop-off and expects work to start immediately and finish by 09:00. The booking confirms intake only. Advisor Leila can offer a status update by 10:00, not a completion guarantee. Omar prefers a phone call. Leila must explain the distinction, acknowledge the timing expectation, and record the call preference. No technician start slot, repair duration, or collection time has been confirmed.
Language workshop: Eight is intake, ten is an update
Communication goal: Correct an appointment misunderstanding with a specific communication promise while keeping workshop start and completion times unconfirmed.
Language guidance: An 08:00 drop-off is not a confirmed 08:00 technician start. Nothing establishes completion by 09:00. Preserve the booked intake while correcting the unsupported assumptions about work and turnaround.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I understand that you expected the work to start at eight. / The 08:00 booking is for drop-off intake. / It does not confirm an 08:00 technician start. / Completion by 09:00 has not been confirmed. / I can provide a status update by 10:00. / That is an update, not a finish guarantee. / You would prefer a phone call. / I will keep phone as the contact method. / We still need actual confirmation before giving a collection time. / This record does not confirm a waiting appointment. / By ten means no later than ten for the update. / Your drop-off booking remains at 08:00. / We have not agreed a separate transport arrangement here. / I cannot promise a work duration that has not been established. / Drop-off at eight; phone update by ten; finish time unconfirmed. / I will keep those three points separate in the booking notes.
Vocabulary: drop-off: Delivery of a vehicle to the service location. / intake: Initial process of receiving the vehicle and recording the request. / appointment: Arranged time for the stated service interaction. / workshop start: Point when the assigned technical work begins. / technician slot: Scheduled period assigned to a technician for work. / completion time: Time when the defined work is expected or confirmed to finish. / collection time: Time agreed for the customer to receive the vehicle. / status update: Communication reporting the current position of the work. / update deadline: Latest time promised for further information. / phone preference: Customer choice to receive communication by telephone. / contact method: Means used to communicate with the customer. / callback number: Number agreed for a return telephone call. / booking terms: Stated conditions defining what an appointment includes. / timing expectation: Customer understanding of when something will happen. / turnaround: Time between receiving work and returning the completed item. / work queue: Ordered or managed set of jobs awaiting attention. / progress report: Account of how far the work has advanced. / estimated finish: Provisional prediction of completion time. / confirmed finish: Completion time explicitly established through the relevant process. / waiting appointment: Booking specifically arranged for a customer to wait under stated terms. / transport arrangement: Plan for the customer's travel while the vehicle is at the shop. / availability window: Period when a person can be contacted or attend. / schedule clarification: Correction of an ambiguous or mistaken timing understanding. / commitment readback: Repetition of agreed promises and their limits.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.
