03Explaining estimates and approval limitsGo ahead with the assessment only+

Your goal

Resolve ambiguous approval by reading back the exact service, charge, and limit, then separate any later repair decision.

This lesson in the book, page 22 ↗

A Read the situation

Go ahead with the assessment only

A fictional shop offers an assessment for $95 under its stated booking terms. No repair is included. Customer Hana says go ahead, intending only the assessment, while advisor Dev needs to record the authorization accurately. Dev must read back the $95 assessment-only scope and route any later repair proposal for separate approval. No repair price, component replacement, or guaranteed diagnostic outcome is supplied. The terms belong to this fictional booking, not to every repair shop.

Hana: Customer / Dev: Service advisor

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does the $95 cover in this booking?

Answer and explanation

D. Assessment only

The stated fictional terms cover assessment and explicitly exclude repair.

2. What does Hana intend by go ahead?

Answer and explanation

B. Permission for the assessment only

The customer intends the limited assessment described in the brief.

3. What must happen with a later repair proposal?

Answer and explanation

A. It needs separate approval

The supplied process requires a separate decision on any later repair proposal.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The fictional booking offers a $95 assessment, with no repair included. Hana intends to approve that service only. The phrase go ahead must not expand into permission for an undefined repair or component replacement.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
assessment fee
Charge for the defined evaluation service.explain the assessment fee
diagnostic time
Time spent investigating a reported vehicle concern.distinguish diagnostic time
assessment scope
Work included in the agreed evaluation.confirm the assessment scope
repair scope
Defined corrective work proposed or authorized.separate the repair scope
authorization
Permission for a specific action or service.record the authorization
approval limit
Boundary of what the customer has agreed to.preserve the approval limit
assessment only
Wording excluding repair from the current approval.state assessment only
repair proposal
Description of suggested corrective work and its relevant terms.present the repair proposal
separate approval
New permission for work outside the current authorization.obtain separate approval
written estimate
Document describing expected cost for defined work.review the written estimate
itemized quote
Price proposal separating relevant components of cost.provide an itemized quote
parts charge
Cost associated with supplied components.identify the parts charge
labor charge
Cost associated with the stated work time or labor provision.distinguish the labor charge
scope readback
Repetition of the exact work covered by permission.give a scope readback
stated terms
Conditions explicitly supplied for the booking or service.follow the stated terms
cost commitment
Agreed financial obligation for defined work.clarify the cost commitment
additional work
Work beyond the already authorized scope.seek approval for additional work
component replacement
Removal and substitution of a particular vehicle part.propose component replacement
diagnostic outcome
Result of the assessment, which may include unresolved questions.avoid guaranteeing a diagnostic outcome
customer consent
Customer agreement to the identified work and terms.confirm customer consent
approval record
Documentation of the permission actually given.update the approval record
unpriced repair
Corrective work for which a price has not been supplied.distinguish an unpriced repair
service inclusion
Item expressly covered in the described service.confirm service inclusions
decision boundary
Limit between the present agreement and a later required decision.explain the decision boundary

Any later repair proposal requires separate approval under the supplied terms. The assessment fee does not establish a repair price or guarantee a particular diagnostic outcome. Keep the present cost and later decision separate.

C Notice the language

Confirm the meaning of a short yes

A brief go ahead can conceal different assumptions about scope. Read back the service and charge in one sentence, then state what is not included. The aim is a shared record of the actual decision, not pressure to approve an unspecified repair.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Name the present service
The $95 charge is for the assessment.
State the limit
No repair is included in that amount.
Clarify the short approval
By go ahead, do you mean the assessment only?
Read back the charge
You are authorizing the $95 assessment.
Preserve the scope
I will record assessment only.
Separate later work
Any later repair proposal will need separate approval.
Avoid an automatic extension
Your current yes does not authorize unspecified repairs.
Avoid a false inclusion
The assessment charge is not a repair package.
Keep the outcome open
We have not guaranteed a particular diagnostic result.
Keep the repair price open
No repair price has been supplied yet.
Ask for confirmation
Does that match the permission you intend to give?
Explain the next decision
We will explain any proposed repair before seeking its approval.
Keep components unapproved
No component replacement is authorized by this assessment approval.
Use the actual terms
These are the stated terms for this booking.
Record the agreement
The approval record will show the service, amount, and limit.
Close without pressure
The later repair decision remains separate from today's assessment.
6 language and precision notes
For the assessment
Attaches the price to one defined service.
Only
Restricts authorization rather than merely emphasizing the service name.
By go ahead, do you mean ...?
Checks the intended meaning of an ambiguous phrase.
Any later
Keeps a future proposal conditional rather than inevitable.
Not included
Identifies a scope boundary without inventing another price.
Separate from
Prevents present consent from silently authorizing later work.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which readback is most accurate?

Answer and explanation

B. You authorize the $95 assessment only; repair needs separate approval

The readback includes the stated service, amount, and authorization boundary.

2. What should Dev avoid promising?

Answer and explanation

D. A particular diagnostic result or unpriced repair

The brief supplies neither a guaranteed outcome nor a repair price.

3. What makes the original go ahead ambiguous?

Answer and explanation

A. It does not itself state the exact approved scope

A short approval phrase needs the agreed service and limit attached.

4. Which item belongs in the approval record?

Answer and explanation

B. Assessment only, $95, with repair excluded

The record should preserve the permission actually given under the stated terms.

Check explanations in the book, page 89 ↗

E Conversations

Go ahead with the assessment only

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Hana: Customer / Dev: Service advisor

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • approval record
  • assessment fee
  • repair proposal
  • component replacement
  • scope readback
  • customer consent
  • approval limit
  • separate approval
  • assessment scope
  • diagnostic outcome
  1. Hana
    Go ahead with the ninety-five dollars. But before you put that through, is that just looking at the problem, or does it include fixing it?
  2. Dev
    It's assessment only. I'll record the 1 with the price so there's no suggestion that a repair is included.
  3. Hana
    Then please don't put just customer says go ahead. Someone else could read that as permission to fit whatever part they find.
  4. Dev
    Agreed. The 2 is the $95 assessment, with no corrective work approved. I'll put that in the workshop instruction.
  5. Hana
    Is ninety-five a deposit against a repair, then? I had understood it as a charge for finding out what's wrong.
  6. Dev
    Here it's the 3 for the evaluation, not a repair allowance. We haven't agreed any later repair price.
  7. Hana
    And if the assessment doesn't identify one definite faulty part, does that mean you haven't done what I've paid for?
  8. Dev
    We haven't guaranteed a particular 4. The authorized service is the assessment; I shouldn't promise a specific finding before it happens.
  9. Hana
    All right. Suppose the technician recommends work. I want to hear what it is and what it costs before deciding.
  10. Dev
    We'll bring any 5 back to you as a separate decision. Today's approval doesn't settle that work or its price.
  11. Hana
    Even a replacement that seems obvious to the technician? I don't want obvious to become already ordered on my behalf.
  12. Dev
    No 6 is authorized under this agreement. A likely recommendation still isn't your permission to carry it out.
  13. Hana
    Can you make that visible to the next advisor? I may not speak to you when the assessment comes back.
  14. Dev
    Yes. The note will require 7 for any repair, so the handover doesn't depend on someone remembering this conversation.
  15. Hana
    Please read that line to me before we finish. I'd like the price and the limit together.
  16. Dev
    My 8 is: you authorize the $95 assessment only; no repair is included or approved. Any later repair proposal requires another decision from you.
  17. Hana
    Yes, that's what I'm agreeing to. Not an open-ended repair, and not a promise that the assessment finds a particular failed part.
  18. Dev
    I'll attach that 9 to this booking, with the assessment-only scope. It won't carry forward as approval for future work.
  19. Hana
    Thank you. Go ahead with the assessment on those terms, and come back to me about anything beyond it.
  20. Dev
    I'll complete the 10 now: assessment, $95, no repair included. The workshop and the next advisor will receive the same instruction.

Answers and explanations
  1. assessment scope Turn 2. Assessment scope defines the evaluation Hana is approving, separate from corrective work.
  2. approval limit Turn 4. Approval limit preserves Hana's permission for assessment without extending it to unspecified repairs.
  3. assessment fee Turn 6. Assessment fee is the stated $95 charge for the defined evaluation service.
  4. diagnostic outcome Turn 8. Diagnostic outcome is not guaranteed by the stated assessment fee or authorization.
  5. repair proposal Turn 10. Repair proposal describes later corrective work and must be presented for a distinct customer decision.
  6. component replacement Turn 12. Component replacement is corrective work outside the present assessment-only authorization.
  7. separate approval Turn 14. Separate approval is required for any later repair outside the current assessment scope.
  8. scope readback Turn 16. Scope readback repeats the service, price, and exclusion so Hana can confirm the actual agreement.
  9. customer consent Turn 18. Customer consent applies to the specific assessment and stated terms, not to future unpriced work.
  10. approval record Turn 20. Approval record documents the present limited permission so the workshop and later advisor receive the same instruction.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Hana: Go ahead with the ninety-five dollars. But before you put that through, is that just looking at the problem, or does it include fixing it?
  2. Dev: It's assessment only. I'll record the assessment scope with the price so there's no suggestion that a repair is included.
  3. Hana: Then please don't put just customer says go ahead. Someone else could read that as permission to fit whatever part they find.
  4. Dev: Agreed. The approval limit is the $95 assessment, with no corrective work approved. I'll put that in the workshop instruction.
  5. Hana: Is ninety-five a deposit against a repair, then? I had understood it as a charge for finding out what's wrong.
  6. Dev: Here it's the assessment fee for the evaluation, not a repair allowance. We haven't agreed any later repair price.
  7. Hana: And if the assessment doesn't identify one definite faulty part, does that mean you haven't done what I've paid for?
  8. Dev: We haven't guaranteed a particular diagnostic outcome. The authorized service is the assessment; I shouldn't promise a specific finding before it happens.
  9. Hana: All right. Suppose the technician recommends work. I want to hear what it is and what it costs before deciding.
  10. Dev: We'll bring any repair proposal back to you as a separate decision. Today's approval doesn't settle that work or its price.
  11. Hana: Even a replacement that seems obvious to the technician? I don't want obvious to become already ordered on my behalf.
  12. Dev: No component replacement is authorized under this agreement. A likely recommendation still isn't your permission to carry it out.
  13. Hana: Can you make that visible to the next advisor? I may not speak to you when the assessment comes back.
  14. Dev: Yes. The note will require separate approval for any repair, so the handover doesn't depend on someone remembering this conversation.
  15. Hana: Please read that line to me before we finish. I'd like the price and the limit together.
  16. Dev: My scope readback is: you authorize the $95 assessment only; no repair is included or approved. Any later repair proposal requires another decision from you.
  17. Hana: Yes, that's what I'm agreeing to. Not an open-ended repair, and not a promise that the assessment finds a particular failed part.
  18. Dev: I'll attach that customer consent to this booking, with the assessment-only scope. It won't carry forward as approval for future work.
  19. Hana: Thank you. Go ahead with the assessment on those terms, and come back to me about anything beyond it.
  20. Dev: I'll complete the approval record now: assessment, $95, no repair included. The workshop and the next advisor will receive the same instruction.
Dialogue in the book, page 28 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Ninety-five dollars for assessment only

An advisor reads back a fictional shop's assessment terms.

  1. Customer: Ninety-five dollars is fine. Go ahead with the assessment, but not a repair.
  2. Advisor: To confirm, your approval covers the stated ninety-five-dollar assessment only under this booking.
  3. Customer: Yes. I want to hear any findings and proposed repair price before deciding.
  4. Advisor: I'll preserve that authorization limit. Additional work would need a separate discussion and decision.
  5. Customer: Does saying go ahead mean the technician can add other work up to that amount?
  6. Advisor: No. Your approval names the assessment scope, not any task costing less than ninety-five.
  7. Customer: Thank you. Please explain that distinction on the repair order for the technician.
  8. Advisor: I will. The diagnostic charge and any later repair proposal will remain separate.
  9. Customer: And the amount you stated applies to this particular assessment, not every service visit?
  10. Advisor: Correct. These are this booking's stated terms; other service scopes have their own terms.
Approval for one proposed item

A customer clarifies a decision after receiving two service proposals.

  1. Advisor: The technician's report includes two proposals, listed separately as item A and item B.
  2. Customer: I approve item A under the quoted terms, but I haven't decided about B.
  3. Advisor: Then I'll record approval for A only, with B awaiting your separate decision.
  4. Customer: Please don't mark the whole estimate approved just because I accepted one line.
  5. Advisor: I won't. Each proposal needs to retain its actual approval status on this record.
  6. Customer: Can I ask more questions about B without that being treated as permission?
  7. Advisor: Yes. I'll arrange an explanation; asking about an item isn't approval to proceed.
  8. Customer: That's helpful. My question is what the recommendation means, not when to start it.
  9. Advisor: I'll pass that question to the technician and leave B's authorization status unchanged.
  10. Customer: Then we agree: A approved as quoted, B unapproved while I seek clarification.
An estimate revision needs explanation

A customer queries two differently dated estimates.

  1. Customer: I have an earlier estimate for two hundred dollars and a newer one for two forty.
  2. Advisor: The newer document includes a proposed additional line. It doesn't establish that you approved it.
  3. Customer: I approved the earlier scope. Please explain what changed before seeking another decision.
  4. Advisor: I'll compare the two versions and identify that added line for your review.
  5. Customer: Does the revised total automatically replace the authorization limit from our earlier conversation?
  6. Advisor: No. We need to clarify the proposed change and your actual decision under the shop's terms.
  7. Customer: Then don't describe my request for an explanation as refusal of every service item.
  8. Advisor: I won't. Your earlier approval and the new proposal are separate parts of this record.
  9. Customer: Please give me the explanation with both dates so I can follow the comparison.
  10. Advisor: I'll arrange that now and preserve the additional line as pending your decision.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Go ahead with the assessment only
  1. Complete the dialogue with the assessment and approval terms.
  2. Read the approval exchange aloud. Include $95, assessment only, repair excluded, and separate approval without dropping any part.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Read back the exact approval

Complete the authorization record using the fictional booking terms. Do not add an unpriced repair.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Advisor: "The authorized charge is ___ dollars."

Answer and explanation

A. 95

Ninety-five dollars is the stated price of the assessment service.

2. Customer: "I approve the ___ only."

Answer and explanation

A. assessment

Assessment is the specific service Hana intends to authorize.

3. Advisor: "No ___ is included or approved."

Answer and explanation

C. repair

Repair falls outside the current assessment-only agreement and stated charge.

4. Customer: "Any later proposal needs ___ approval."

Answer and explanation

A. separate

Separate approval preserves a distinct decision for any later repair proposal.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Course: Automotive Service English

Lesson 03: Explaining estimates and approval limits
Original fictional case: A fictional shop offers an assessment for $95 under its stated booking terms. No repair is included. Customer Hana says go ahead, intending only the assessment, while advisor Dev needs to record the authorization accurately. Dev must read back the $95 assessment-only scope and route any later repair proposal for separate approval. No repair price, component replacement, or guaranteed diagnostic outcome is supplied. The terms belong to this fictional booking, not to every repair shop.
Language workshop: Go ahead with the assessment only
Communication goal: Resolve ambiguous approval by reading back the exact service, charge, and limit, then separate any later repair decision.
Language guidance: The fictional booking offers a $95 assessment, with no repair included. Hana intends to approve that service only. The phrase go ahead must not expand into permission for an undefined repair or component replacement.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The $95 charge is for the assessment. / No repair is included in that amount. / By go ahead, do you mean the assessment only? / You are authorizing the $95 assessment. / I will record assessment only. / Any later repair proposal will need separate approval. / Your current yes does not authorize unspecified repairs. / The assessment charge is not a repair package. / We have not guaranteed a particular diagnostic result. / No repair price has been supplied yet. / Does that match the permission you intend to give? / We will explain any proposed repair before seeking its approval. / No component replacement is authorized by this assessment approval. / These are the stated terms for this booking. / The approval record will show the service, amount, and limit. / The later repair decision remains separate from today's assessment.
Vocabulary: assessment fee: Charge for the defined evaluation service. / diagnostic time: Time spent investigating a reported vehicle concern. / assessment scope: Work included in the agreed evaluation. / repair scope: Defined corrective work proposed or authorized. / authorization: Permission for a specific action or service. / approval limit: Boundary of what the customer has agreed to. / assessment only: Wording excluding repair from the current approval. / repair proposal: Description of suggested corrective work and its relevant terms. / separate approval: New permission for work outside the current authorization. / written estimate: Document describing expected cost for defined work. / itemized quote: Price proposal separating relevant components of cost. / parts charge: Cost associated with supplied components. / labor charge: Cost associated with the stated work time or labor provision. / scope readback: Repetition of the exact work covered by permission. / stated terms: Conditions explicitly supplied for the booking or service. / cost commitment: Agreed financial obligation for defined work. / additional work: Work beyond the already authorized scope. / component replacement: Removal and substitution of a particular vehicle part. / diagnostic outcome: Result of the assessment, which may include unresolved questions. / customer consent: Customer agreement to the identified work and terms. / approval record: Documentation of the permission actually given. / unpriced repair: Corrective work for which a price has not been supplied. / service inclusion: Item expressly covered in the described service. / decision boundary: Limit between the present agreement and a later required decision.
Speaking task: Complete the dialogue with the assessment and approval terms. Read the approval exchange aloud. Include $95, assessment only, repair excluded, and separate approval without dropping any part. Complete the transfer, check the key, and reread the corrected approval in full.
Partner: Hana: Customer / Dev: Service advisor
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the authorization record using the fictional booking terms. Do not add an unpriced repair.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Hana: Yes, that's what I'm agreeing to. Not an open-ended repair, and not a promise that the assessment finds a particular failed part.
Dev: I'll attach that customer consent to this booking, with the assessment-only scope. It won't carry forward as approval for future work.
Hana: Thank you. Go ahead with the assessment on those terms, and come back to me about anything beyond it.
Dev: I'll complete the approval record now: assessment, $95, no repair included. The workshop and the next advisor will receive the same instruction.
END REFERENCE

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

04Handing the concern to the technicianJob 208: keep the concern and contact limits together+

Your goal

Deliver a concise internal handoff that preserves the symptom, assessment-only authorization, reproduction status, and customer contact window.

This lesson in the book, page 31 ↗

A Read the situation

Job 208: keep the concern and contact limits together

Job card 208 records a customer-reported intermittent cabin rattle after overnight parking. The customer has approved assessment only, is unavailable from 12:00 to 14:00, and prefers a phone call after 14:00. Technician Arun has not reproduced the concern. Advisor Rosa hands over these facts without naming a cause, expanding authorization, or treating non-reproduction as proof that nothing is wrong. The agreed contact window must remain visible with the technical request.

Rosa: Service advisor / Arun: Technician

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which job is being handed over?

Answer and explanation

A. Job card 208

The brief identifies 208 as the record for this internal handoff.

2. What is the authorization?

Answer and explanation

B. Assessment only

The customer has approved only assessment, not unspecified corrective work.

3. What contact arrangement is supplied?

Answer and explanation

D. Phone call after 14:00; unavailable 12:00-14:00

The brief states both the unavailable interval and the preferred later phone contact.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Job 208 concerns a customer-reported cabin rattle after overnight parking. Arun has not reproduced it. Non-reproduction is a limited observation, not proof that the report was mistaken or that a cause has been found.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
internal handoff
Transfer of relevant information between colleagues.give an internal handoff
job identifier
Number or code distinguishing a workshop record.state the job identifier
cabin
Interior space used by vehicle occupants.locate the cabin concern
intermittent rattle
Occasional repeated light knocking or vibrating sound.retain the intermittent rattle description
customer-reported
Attributed to the customer's account rather than a technician finding.label the concern customer-reported
reproduced concern
Reported condition observed again during relevant assessment.record whether the concern was reproduced
not reproduced
Wording stating that the reported condition has not been observed during the assessment.retain not-reproduced status
assessment authorization
Permission for the defined evaluation work.confirm assessment authorization
repair authorization
Permission for specified corrective work.distinguish repair authorization
contact window
Period when customer communication is appropriate under the stated arrangement.preserve the contact window
unavailable interval
Period during which the customer cannot be reached as arranged.record the unavailable interval
preferred channel
Communication method selected by the customer.preserve the preferred channel
workshop handover
Exchange passing a job and its relevant context to the workshop.complete the workshop handover
finding status
Current state of observed or established results.clarify the finding status
diagnostic hypothesis
Possible explanation requiring relevant investigation.distinguish a diagnostic hypothesis
confirmed cause
Explanation established by the relevant assessment.avoid inventing a confirmed cause
noise concern
Customer report about an unwanted vehicle sound.describe the noise concern
reported trigger
Circumstance the customer associates with the symptom.preserve the reported trigger
concern history
Record of earlier descriptions or occurrences.retain the concern history
contact note
Record of communication preferences or restrictions.include the contact note
authorization boundary
Limit of work for which permission has been given.state the authorization boundary
handoff readback
Repetition by the receiving person of the essential information.request a handoff readback
non-reproduction
Absence of the reported symptom during the relevant assessment.distinguish non-reproduction from resolution
next communication
Following planned exchange with the customer.plan the next communication

Assessment-only approval remains in force while the customer is unavailable from 12:00 to 14:00. That interval does not permit extra work. The preferred next contact is a phone call after 14:00, not an invented fixed appointment.

C Notice the language

Treat contact details as part of a technical handoff

A workshop handoff can fail even when the symptom is described accurately if the approval limit or customer availability is lost. Put the job identifier first, separate reported and observed information, then read back permission and contact constraints before ending the exchange.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Open with the identifier
This handoff is for job card 208.
State the customer's concern
The customer reports an intermittent cabin rattle.
Preserve the condition
They associate it with overnight parking.
State the assessment limit
You have not reproduced the concern.
Avoid dismissing the account
Not reproduced does not mean the report is false.
Keep cause open
No confirmed cause is supplied.
State the permission
The authorization is for assessment only.
Exclude assumed extra work
No repair authorization is recorded.
Give the unavailable interval
The customer is unavailable from 12:00 to 14:00.
Give the preferred contact
They prefer a phone call after 14:00.
Avoid an exact-time invention
After two is a window, not a confirmed call at exactly two.
Keep permission unchanged
Being unable to reach the customer does not expand the scope.
Keep the channel visible
Please retain phone as the preferred channel.
Request the receiver's check
Can you read back the concern, scope, and contact window?
Keep observations attributed
Separate the customer report from what you have observed.
Close with a complete handoff
Job 208, assessment only, concern not reproduced, phone after 14:00.
6 language and precision notes
Customer reports
Preserves attribution when the technician has not observed the concern.
Not reproduced
Describes an assessment limit without dismissing an intermittent report.
After versus at
Distinguishes a time window from an exact appointment.
Unavailable from ... to
States both endpoints of the contact restriction.
Does not expand
Keeps permission unchanged despite a communication difficulty.
Concern, scope, contact
Groups three different kinds of essential handoff information.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which handoff preserves all critical facts?

Answer and explanation

D. Job 208: intermittent cabin rattle after overnight parking; assessment only; not reproduced; phone after 14:00

The full handoff retains identifier, concern, condition, permission, status, and contact arrangement.

2. What does not reproduced mean here?

Answer and explanation

C. Arun has not observed the reported concern during assessment

Non-reproduction describes the technician's observation limit, not the truth of the customer's history.

3. When should the preferred phone contact occur?

Answer and explanation

C. After 14:00, outside the stated unavailable interval

The supplied preference is a phone call after the unavailable interval ends.

4. What changes the approval limit during customer unavailability?

Answer and explanation

D. Nothing in these facts

The assessment-only authorization remains unchanged by the customer's contact restriction.

Check explanations in the book, page 91 ↗

E Conversations

Job 208: keep the concern and contact limits together

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Rosa: Service advisor / Arun: Technician

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • unavailable interval
  • authorization boundary
  • confirmed cause
  • contact window
  • handoff readback
  • job identifier
  • preferred channel
  • Non-reproduction
  • customer-reported
  • Repair authorization
  1. Rosa
    Arun, can we check 208 before I call the customer? The desk needs your result and the contact restriction, not just rattle checked.
  2. Arun
    Yes, the 1 is 208. Is this the intermittent cabin rattle after overnight parking?
  3. Rosa
    That's the customer's description. Did you reproduce it, or is that still open?
  4. Arun
    It's still 2. I haven't reproduced the concern, and I don't have a confirmed component diagnosis to add.
  5. Rosa
    Then I shouldn't tell them nothing is wrong. They were quite clear that it doesn't happen every time.
  6. Arun
    Correct. 3 means I haven't observed it in this assessment. It doesn't prove the sound never occurs or that we've repaired it.
  7. Rosa
    Have you done any repair work? The customer approved assessment only, and that's what I have on the desk record.
  8. Arun
    That 4 hasn't changed. There's no approved corrective work, and a possible next step wouldn't create permission to do it.
  9. Rosa
    They're unavailable from noon until two. Please keep that beside the assessment result so nobody tries to contact them in that interval.
  10. Arun
    I'll retain the 5 as 12:00-14:00. The result and that restriction will go back together.
  11. Rosa
    They asked for a phone call after two. I haven't promised a call at two exactly.
  12. Arun
    Understood. The 6 is after 14:00, not a fixed 14:00 appointment. I'll keep the wording as supplied.
  13. Rosa
    The note currently says contact later. Can you include phone? An email wouldn't match their preference.
  14. Arun
    Yes, phone is the 7. I'll include both the method and the after-two window in the return note.
  15. Rosa
    And if we can't reach them, assessment-only still applies. I don't want silence treated as a yes to repair.
  16. Arun
    Agreed. 8 is absent. Difficulty reaching the customer doesn't extend the work they've approved.
  17. Rosa
    Give me the whole handoff once, please, so I can check it against the desk entry.
  18. Arun
    Job 208: intermittent cabin rattle reported after overnight parking, not reproduced, no 9. Assessment only; unavailable 12:00-14:00; phone preferred after 14:00.
  19. Rosa
    That matches. I'll use not reproduced, not no fault exists, and won't turn the contact window into a promised appointment.
  20. Arun
    I'll save that 10 with the job. You can use the same facts without filling in a cause, repair, or call time we haven't established.

Answers and explanations
  1. job identifier Turn 2. Job identifier anchors the handoff to record 208 rather than another workshop job.
  2. customer-reported Turn 4. Customer-reported attributes the intermittent rattle to the owner's account rather than a verified technician finding.
  3. Non-reproduction Turn 6. Non-reproduction is a limited assessment result and does not prove the reported intermittent condition never occurs.
  4. authorization boundary Turn 8. Authorization boundary limits the approved work to assessment rather than corrective action.
  5. unavailable interval Turn 10. Unavailable interval is the stated 12:00-14:00 period that must remain in the contact notes.
  6. contact window Turn 12. Contact window begins after 14:00 but is not an exact appointment at 14:00.
  7. preferred channel Turn 14. Preferred channel is a telephone call, which must not be replaced with an assumed email update.
  8. Repair authorization Turn 16. Repair authorization is absent and is not created by difficulty contacting the customer.
  9. confirmed cause Turn 18. Confirmed cause has not been established, so it must not appear as an assumed diagnosis in the readback.
  10. handoff readback Turn 20. Handoff readback checks the complete transfer of technical and communication facts between colleagues.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Rosa: Arun, can we check 208 before I call the customer? The desk needs your result and the contact restriction, not just rattle checked.
  2. Arun: Yes, the job identifier is 208. Is this the intermittent cabin rattle after overnight parking?
  3. Rosa: That's the customer's description. Did you reproduce it, or is that still open?
  4. Arun: It's still customer-reported. I haven't reproduced the concern, and I don't have a confirmed component diagnosis to add.
  5. Rosa: Then I shouldn't tell them nothing is wrong. They were quite clear that it doesn't happen every time.
  6. Arun: Correct. Non-reproduction means I haven't observed it in this assessment. It doesn't prove the sound never occurs or that we've repaired it.
  7. Rosa: Have you done any repair work? The customer approved assessment only, and that's what I have on the desk record.
  8. Arun: That authorization boundary hasn't changed. There's no approved corrective work, and a possible next step wouldn't create permission to do it.
  9. Rosa: They're unavailable from noon until two. Please keep that beside the assessment result so nobody tries to contact them in that interval.
  10. Arun: I'll retain the unavailable interval as 12:00-14:00. The result and that restriction will go back together.
  11. Rosa: They asked for a phone call after two. I haven't promised a call at two exactly.
  12. Arun: Understood. The contact window is after 14:00, not a fixed 14:00 appointment. I'll keep the wording as supplied.
  13. Rosa: The note currently says contact later. Can you include phone? An email wouldn't match their preference.
  14. Arun: Yes, phone is the preferred channel. I'll include both the method and the after-two window in the return note.
  15. Rosa: And if we can't reach them, assessment-only still applies. I don't want silence treated as a yes to repair.
  16. Arun: Agreed. Repair authorization is absent. Difficulty reaching the customer doesn't extend the work they've approved.
  17. Rosa: Give me the whole handoff once, please, so I can check it against the desk entry.
  18. Arun: Job 208: intermittent cabin rattle reported after overnight parking, not reproduced, no confirmed cause. Assessment only; unavailable 12:00-14:00; phone preferred after 14:00.
  19. Rosa: That matches. I'll use not reproduced, not no fault exists, and won't turn the contact window into a promised appointment.
  20. Arun: I'll save that handoff readback with the job. You can use the same facts without filling in a cause, repair, or call time we haven't established.
Dialogue in the book, page 37 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Job card 208

An advisor hands an assessment-only job to a technician.

  1. Advisor: Job card two oh eight is assessment only for a reported intermittent cabin rattle.
  2. Technician: Has another technician reproduced it, or is the description entirely from the customer?
  3. Advisor: It is customer-reported after overnight parking; no technician finding is recorded yet.
  4. Technician: I'll keep that report distinct from whatever this assessment actually establishes or doesn't establish.
  5. Advisor: The customer is unavailable from noon to two and prefers a call afterward.
  6. Technician: Thanks. Any additional work proposal should go through the service desk for discussion.
  7. Advisor: Correct. Their contact preference doesn't extend the currently agreed scope on this job.
  8. Technician: Does the previous repair order concern the same symptom or a different visit?
  9. Advisor: It concerns a display issue; don't merge that history with today's reported rattle.
  10. Technician: I have the scope, source, and contact window. I'll return findings against this job card.
A coolant observation in a carryover job

A technician hands a stated finding to the incoming service advisor without prescribing work.

  1. Technician: This carryover job includes my recorded coolant observation, but no repair is approved.
  2. Advisor: Please distinguish your actual finding from the customer's original report before I call them.
  3. Technician: The customer reported a visible mark. My note records coolant residue at the inspected location.
  4. Advisor: Has that established a particular faulty component, or does the cause remain unconfirmed?
  5. Technician: No component fault is confirmed. Please relay the observation without adding a repair recommendation.
  6. Advisor: I can explain that coolant is fluid used in the vehicle's cooling system.
  7. Technician: Yes, but refer the observation's significance to me; don't advise the customer on handling it.
  8. Advisor: I'll keep the technical explanation separate from any proposed additional work or charge.
  9. Technician: The service desk owns the callback. No completion estimate has been confirmed for this carryover.
  10. Advisor: I'll report the stated finding and ask you to explain the remaining uncertainty directly.
The customer's hypothesis was copied as a finding

An advisor corrects a job-card wording error with a technician.

  1. Advisor: The job card says faulty speaker, but that was the customer's suggestion, not a finding.
  2. Technician: Thanks for flagging it. What did the customer actually report experiencing in the vehicle?
  3. Advisor: An occasional buzz near the door. They wondered whether the speaker might be involved.
  4. Technician: Then the symptom description is the buzz; the speaker explanation remains their unverified hypothesis.
  5. Advisor: I'll correct the source wording and retain both pieces without treating the suggestion as established.
  6. Technician: Please notify the service desk too, since they may have seen the stronger wording already.
  7. Advisor: I will. Updating the card doesn't guarantee earlier readers know the claim was corrected.
  8. Technician: Exactly. We need the handoff to distinguish customer-reported condition from technician finding.
  9. Advisor: No assessment result exists yet. I'll make that explicit in the corrected message.
  10. Technician: That gives me an accurate starting point without presupposing a cause for the reported sound.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Job 208: keep the concern and contact limits together
  1. Complete the workshop handoff using the supplied terms.
  2. Read the handoff aloud, preserving not reproduced, assessment only, unavailable 12:00-14:00, and phone after 14:00.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Pass on the concern, scope, and contact

Complete the internal handoff. Keep the job identifier and contact window attached to the assessment-only scope.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Advisor: "This is job card ___."

Answer and explanation

D. 208

The handoff concerns record 208, not another service job.

2. Technician: "The approval covers ___ only."

Answer and explanation

A. assessment

Assessment is the stated authorization and does not include repair.

3. Advisor: "The customer is unavailable from 12:00 to ___."

Answer and explanation

C. 14:00

Fourteen hundred is the end of the supplied unavailable interval.

4. Technician: "They prefer a ___ call afterward."

Answer and explanation

D. phone

Phone preserves the requested contact method after the unavailable period.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Automotive Service English
Professional audience: Service reception staff, service advisors, automotive technicians, parts-counter staff, and workshop assistants
Scope: The vehicles, shop terms, charges, records, and events are fictional. This book teaches English, not vehicle diagnosis, repair, testing, driving decisions, or legal advice. Follow actual training, authorization, manufacturer information, shop procedures, and applicable requirements. A code, invoice, or customer description does not establish vehicle safety, readiness, repair necessity, or warranty coverage. The dialogues give no instructions to reproduce symptoms while driving or work on a vehicle.
Lesson 04: Handing the concern to the technician
Original fictional case: Job card 208 records a customer-reported intermittent cabin rattle after overnight parking. The customer has approved assessment only, is unavailable from 12:00 to 14:00, and prefers a phone call after 14:00. Technician Arun has not reproduced the concern. Advisor Rosa hands over these facts without naming a cause, expanding authorization, or treating non-reproduction as proof that nothing is wrong. The agreed contact window must remain visible with the technical request.
Language workshop: Job 208: keep the concern and contact limits together
Communication goal: Deliver a concise internal handoff that preserves the symptom, assessment-only authorization, reproduction status, and customer contact window.
Language guidance: Job 208 concerns a customer-reported cabin rattle after overnight parking. Arun has not reproduced it. Non-reproduction is a limited observation, not proof that the report was mistaken or that a cause has been found.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): This handoff is for job card 208. / The customer reports an intermittent cabin rattle. / They associate it with overnight parking. / You have not reproduced the concern. / Not reproduced does not mean the report is false. / No confirmed cause is supplied. / The authorization is for assessment only. / No repair authorization is recorded. / The customer is unavailable from 12:00 to 14:00. / They prefer a phone call after 14:00. / After two is a window, not a confirmed call at exactly two. / Being unable to reach the customer does not expand the scope. / Please retain phone as the preferred channel. / Can you read back the concern, scope, and contact window? / Separate the customer report from what you have observed. / Job 208, assessment only, concern not reproduced, phone after 14:00.
Vocabulary: internal handoff: Transfer of relevant information between colleagues. / job identifier: Number or code distinguishing a workshop record. / cabin: Interior space used by vehicle occupants. / intermittent rattle: Occasional repeated light knocking or vibrating sound. / customer-reported: Attributed to the customer's account rather than a technician finding. / reproduced concern: Reported condition observed again during relevant assessment. / not reproduced: Wording stating that the reported condition has not been observed during the assessment. / assessment authorization: Permission for the defined evaluation work. / repair authorization: Permission for specified corrective work. / contact window: Period when customer communication is appropriate under the stated arrangement. / unavailable interval: Period during which the customer cannot be reached as arranged. / preferred channel: Communication method selected by the customer. / workshop handover: Exchange passing a job and its relevant context to the workshop. / finding status: Current state of observed or established results. / diagnostic hypothesis: Possible explanation requiring relevant investigation. / confirmed cause: Explanation established by the relevant assessment. / noise concern: Customer report about an unwanted vehicle sound. / reported trigger: Circumstance the customer associates with the symptom. / concern history: Record of earlier descriptions or occurrences. / contact note: Record of communication preferences or restrictions. / authorization boundary: Limit of work for which permission has been given. / handoff readback: Repetition by the receiving person of the essential information. / non-reproduction: Absence of the reported symptom during the relevant assessment. / next communication: Following planned exchange with the customer.
Speaking task: Complete the workshop handoff using the supplied terms. Read the handoff aloud, preserving not reproduced, assessment only, unavailable 12:00-14:00, and phone after 14:00. Complete the transfer and check the key. Repeat the handoff without turning after two into at two.
Partner: Rosa: Service advisor / Arun: Technician
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the internal handoff. Keep the job identifier and contact window attached to the assessment-only scope.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Rosa: Give me the whole handoff once, please, so I can check it against the desk entry.
Arun: Job 208: intermittent cabin rattle reported after overnight parking, not reproduced, no confirmed cause. Assessment only; unavailable 12:00-14:00; phone preferred after 14:00.
Rosa: That matches. I'll use not reproduced, not no fault exists, and won't turn the contact window into a promised appointment.
Arun: I'll save that handoff readback with the job. You can use the same facts without filling in a cause, repair, or call time we haven't established.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

END REFERENCE
05Explaining parts and completion delaysTuesday arrival is not Tuesday collection+

Your goal

Explain a parts delay by separating supplier estimates, shipment confirmation, workshop scheduling, and a firm update commitment.

This lesson in the book, page 40 ↗

A Read the situation

Tuesday arrival is not Tuesday collection

The part required for job 315 is on back order. The supplier estimates arrival on Tuesday, but shipment is not confirmed. Customer Ben wants to collect the vehicle on Tuesday. Technician scheduling and completion are also unconfirmed. Parts clerk Nia promises a supply-status update on Monday afternoon. She must acknowledge the requested pickup without accepting it, preserve the supplier estimate as provisional, and avoid treating part arrival as a completed repair.

Ben: Customer / Nia: Parts clerk

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is the part's current status?

Answer and explanation

D. On back order with shipment unconfirmed

The part is back-ordered, and no shipment confirmation has been supplied.

2. What does Tuesday represent?

Answer and explanation

C. The supplier's estimated arrival

Tuesday is a provisional supply estimate rather than a workshop or collection commitment.

3. What does Nia promise?

Answer and explanation

C. A supply-status update Monday afternoon

Nia commits to an update, not to a supply outcome or completed repair.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Tuesday is the supplier estimate for part arrival. Shipment is unconfirmed, and the part is on back order. Keep the estimate provisional rather than changing it to a guaranteed delivery or a booked collection.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
back order
Order for an item not currently available for immediate supply.confirm back-order status
supplier estimate
Provisional timing or quantity information given by the supplier.qualify the supplier estimate
estimated arrival
Expected delivery time not necessarily confirmed.state the estimated arrival
shipment confirmation
Evidence that goods have been dispatched under the stated arrangement.await shipment confirmation
dispatch
Sending goods from the supplier or distribution point.distinguish dispatch from arrival
delivery
Transfer of goods to the destination.confirm delivery
lead time
Time between an order stage and the relevant supply stage.clarify the lead time
parts availability
Whether the required item can be supplied at the relevant time.check parts availability
part allocation
Assignment of available stock to a particular order or job.verify part allocation
order status
Current position of a purchase or supply request.update the order status
supply-status update
Communication about progress or uncertainty in obtaining an item.provide a supply-status update
workshop scheduling
Assignment of technical work to available time and staff.confirm workshop scheduling
installation
Fitting the relevant component as part of authorized work.distinguish installation from delivery
completion confirmation
Verified statement that the relevant work has finished.obtain completion confirmation
pickup request
Customer preference for collecting the vehicle at a stated time.record the pickup request
collection commitment
Agreed promise about when the vehicle can be handed over.distinguish a collection commitment
dependency
Prior condition on which a later stage relies.explain the dependency
provisional date
Date subject to confirmation or change.retain the provisional date
confirmed date
Date established through the relevant process.distinguish a confirmed date
parts clerk
Staff member coordinating component identification and supply.contact the parts clerk
delay notice
Communication explaining a change or uncertainty in timing.give a delay notice
supplier follow-up
Further contact to clarify supply information.arrange supplier follow-up
progress checkpoint
Agreed point for reviewing and communicating status.set a progress checkpoint
uncertain completion
Finish status or timing that has not been established.explain uncertain completion

Even part arrival would not by itself confirm technician scheduling, installation, or completion. Nia promises a Monday-afternoon supply update. Ben requests Tuesday pickup, but that request has not been accepted as a commitment.

C Notice the language

Give the dependency chain in ordinary language

A customer may hear a part date as a vehicle-ready date. Separate supply, delivery, workshop work, and collection in a short sequence. Preserve the useful supplier estimate but label it honestly, then give a definite communication commitment that does not depend on a guessed result.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
State the current supply position
The part for job 315 is on back order.
Attribute the date
The supplier estimates arrival on Tuesday.
Preserve uncertainty
Shipment has not been confirmed.
Separate dispatch and arrival
A dispatch confirmation would not itself mean the part has arrived.
Acknowledge the request
I understand that you would like Tuesday pickup.
Avoid accepting the date
I cannot confirm Tuesday collection from the current information.
Name the next dependency
Technician scheduling still needs confirmation.
Keep completion separate
Part arrival is not the same as completed work.
Give the firm update
I will update you on supply status Monday afternoon.
Limit the promise
That is a communication commitment, not a guaranteed supply result.
Avoid inventing allocation
We have no confirmed allocation or shipment to report here.
Retain the job reference
Please keep job 315 attached to this parts update.
Distinguish expected and confirmed
Tuesday remains estimated, not confirmed.
Avoid a guaranteed turnaround
The completion time is still unconfirmed.
Read back the chain
Back order, estimated Tuesday arrival, shipment and completion unconfirmed.
Close with the next contact
You will hear from me Monday afternoon about the supply position.
6 language and precision notes
Supplier estimates
Attributes a provisional date to its actual source.
Would like
Records a customer preference without accepting it as agreed.
Not the same as
Separates supply stages from workshop completion.
Still needs confirmation
Keeps a remaining dependency visible.
Will update
Commits to communication rather than the hoped-for outcome.
Estimated, not confirmed
Preserves the level of certainty attached to the date.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which statement preserves the supplier information?

Answer and explanation

A. Arrival is estimated for Tuesday, with shipment unconfirmed

The estimate is provisional and does not establish shipment or collection.

2. Why can Nia not promise Tuesday pickup?

Answer and explanation

C. Supply, technician scheduling, and completion are not confirmed

The necessary supply and workshop stages remain unresolved in the brief.

3. What should Nia promise in this case?

Answer and explanation

A. A supply-status update Monday afternoon

The supplied commitment is a status update at the stated time.

4. What does Ben's request establish?

Answer and explanation

D. His preferred collection date, not an accepted promise

A requested pickup date must remain distinct from a confirmed collection commitment.

E Conversations

Tuesday arrival is not Tuesday collection

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Ben: Customer / Nia: Parts clerk

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • supply-status update
  • pickup request
  • collection commitment
  • back order
  • completion confirmation
  • order status
  • Workshop scheduling
  • progress checkpoint
  • shipment confirmation
  • estimated arrival
  1. Ben
    I was told Tuesday for the part on job 315. Can I arrange to collect the vehicle that afternoon?
  2. Nia
    The part is on 1. The supplier has estimated Tuesday arrival, but shipment is not confirmed, so I cannot describe Tuesday collection as an agreed plan.
  3. Ben
    So Tuesday is the supplier's estimate, and you don't have a shipment confirmation yet?
  4. Nia
    We are still waiting for 2. I should keep the supplier estimate separate from evidence of dispatch rather than let the date sound more definite than it is.
  5. Ben
    Even if it arrives then, does that automatically put my vehicle into the workshop on Tuesday?
  6. Nia
    No. 3 is not confirmed either. Part arrival, the work slot, and completion are distinct stages; the supply estimate does not establish all of them.
  7. Ben
    Tuesday pickup is what I'd like, but I understand you haven't accepted it as a booking.
  8. Nia
    I understand your 4 for Tuesday. I will keep it in the notes as your preference, not as a confirmed handover date.
  9. Ben
    When will you check again and contact me? I'd rather have a clear update than keep calling.
  10. Nia
    I will provide a 5 on Monday afternoon. That is my commitment to contact you, even though I cannot promise what the supplier's answer will be.
  11. Ben
    Monday afternoon for the supply update. That doesn't mean the part will have shipped by then.
  12. Nia
    Exactly. It is a 6, not a guaranteed dispatch or arrival. The update should distinguish what is confirmed from what remains estimated at that time.
  13. Ben
    Please don't change the collection request to confirmed in the notes. I need a real finish time before arranging transport.
  14. Nia
    A 7 needs the relevant completion and handover confirmation. We do not yet have that, and I would not want your travel plans built on an unsupported promise.
  15. Ben
    Can you keep job 315 on the parts query, so the next advisor sees the same order?
  16. Nia
    Certainly. Job 315 will stay attached to the 8 information. The note will identify the back order, the supplier's Tuesday estimate, and the missing shipment confirmation.
  17. Ben
    Read back the supplier estimate for me once. I'd been treating Tuesday as a promise.
  18. Nia
    The 9 is Tuesday; shipment is unconfirmed. Technician scheduling and completion are also unconfirmed. I will update you about supply on Monday afternoon.
  19. Ben
    All right. I'll expect your Monday-afternoon update, with collection still unconfirmed.
  20. Nia
    Correct. We will keep 10 separate from the parts forecast. You have a definite next update, but no invented guarantee about delivery, installation, or pickup.

Answers and explanations
  1. back order Turn 2. Back order is the current supply status; the part is not confirmed as immediately available.
  2. shipment confirmation Turn 4. Shipment confirmation is absent, so an actual dispatch must not be implied.
  3. Workshop scheduling Turn 6. Workshop scheduling remains unconfirmed and is a separate stage from the supplier's estimated arrival.
  4. pickup request Turn 8. Pickup request records Ben's preference for Tuesday without making it an accepted collection commitment.
  5. supply-status update Turn 10. Supply-status update is the Monday-afternoon communication Nia actually promises.
  6. progress checkpoint Turn 12. Progress checkpoint identifies an agreed communication point, not a shipment or completion guarantee.
  7. collection commitment Turn 14. Collection commitment requires actual readiness arrangements and is not established by a supplier estimate.
  8. order status Turn 16. Order status records the supply position for the specific job rather than a general parts conversation.
  9. estimated arrival Turn 18. Estimated arrival is the supplier's provisional Tuesday date, not a confirmed delivery or finished vehicle.
  10. completion confirmation Turn 20. Completion confirmation is the later verified status needed before a collection promise can be made.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Ben: I was told Tuesday for the part on job 315. Can I arrange to collect the vehicle that afternoon?
  2. Nia: The part is on back order. The supplier has estimated Tuesday arrival, but shipment is not confirmed, so I cannot describe Tuesday collection as an agreed plan.
  3. Ben: So Tuesday is the supplier's estimate, and you don't have a shipment confirmation yet?
  4. Nia: We are still waiting for shipment confirmation. I should keep the supplier estimate separate from evidence of dispatch rather than let the date sound more definite than it is.
  5. Ben: Even if it arrives then, does that automatically put my vehicle into the workshop on Tuesday?
  6. Nia: No. Workshop scheduling is not confirmed either. Part arrival, the work slot, and completion are distinct stages; the supply estimate does not establish all of them.
  7. Ben: Tuesday pickup is what I'd like, but I understand you haven't accepted it as a booking.
  8. Nia: I understand your pickup request for Tuesday. I will keep it in the notes as your preference, not as a confirmed handover date.
  9. Ben: When will you check again and contact me? I'd rather have a clear update than keep calling.
  10. Nia: I will provide a supply-status update on Monday afternoon. That is my commitment to contact you, even though I cannot promise what the supplier's answer will be.
  11. Ben: Monday afternoon for the supply update. That doesn't mean the part will have shipped by then.
  12. Nia: Exactly. It is a progress checkpoint, not a guaranteed dispatch or arrival. The update should distinguish what is confirmed from what remains estimated at that time.
  13. Ben: Please don't change the collection request to confirmed in the notes. I need a real finish time before arranging transport.
  14. Nia: A collection commitment needs the relevant completion and handover confirmation. We do not yet have that, and I would not want your travel plans built on an unsupported promise.
  15. Ben: Can you keep job 315 on the parts query, so the next advisor sees the same order?
  16. Nia: Certainly. Job 315 will stay attached to the order status information. The note will identify the back order, the supplier's Tuesday estimate, and the missing shipment confirmation.
  17. Ben: Read back the supplier estimate for me once. I'd been treating Tuesday as a promise.
  18. Nia: The estimated arrival is Tuesday; shipment is unconfirmed. Technician scheduling and completion are also unconfirmed. I will update you about supply on Monday afternoon.
  19. Ben: All right. I'll expect your Monday-afternoon update, with collection still unconfirmed.
  20. Nia: Correct. We will keep completion confirmation separate from the parts forecast. You have a definite next update, but no invented guarantee about delivery, installation, or pickup.
Dialogue in the book, page 46 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Tuesday is the part estimate

An advisor updates a customer about job 315.

  1. Advisor: The part for job three fifteen is on back order, with Tuesday estimated by the supplier.
  2. Customer: Does that mean I can collect the car Tuesday afternoon once it arrives?
  3. Advisor: No. Tuesday describes estimated parts delivery, not confirmed arrival or completed vehicle work.
  4. Customer: I need transport arrangements, so please don't leave the distinction hidden in the update.
  5. Advisor: The parts desk will give us another supply-status update Monday afternoon, even if availability remains uncertain.
  6. Customer: After that, can the service team review the completion estimate using the confirmed information?
  7. Advisor: Yes. They'll need supply status and workshop scheduling before giving a supported completion estimate.
  8. Customer: Then I should treat Tuesday pickup as unconfirmed rather than reserve a ride around it.
  9. Advisor: Correct. I'll keep the part estimate, next update, and vehicle readiness as separate stages.
  10. Customer: Thank you. A clear uncertainty is more useful than an arrival date presented as a pickup promise.
A label exists, but dispatch is unconfirmed

A parts clerk clarifies shipment evidence with an advisor.

  1. Advisor: The tracking page shows a label created. Can I tell the customer the part has shipped?
  2. Parts clerk: Not from that status alone. We haven't confirmed that the carrier received the actual package.
  3. Advisor: Then label creation is a preparation event, not proof of the supplier's dispatch?
  4. Parts clerk: Exactly. I'll ask for shipment confirmation and keep the estimated delivery date qualified.
  5. Advisor: The customer already knows a part was ordered; they need the current supply stage.
  6. Parts clerk: You can say ordered, tracking label created, carrier possession not yet confirmed.
  7. Advisor: I'll avoid calling it in transit until the tracking or supplier confirms that movement.
  8. Parts clerk: Please do. The completion estimate also remains dependent on arrival and the remaining workshop schedule.
  9. Advisor: Can you send the confirmed update to the service desk once the supplier responds?
  10. Parts clerk: Yes. I'll identify the part request and actual shipment status, not just forward an unexplained tracking number.
One part available, another pending

An advisor and parts clerk clarify a mixed supply status.

  1. Advisor: The parts request names a battery terminal and a separate cover. Are both available?
  2. Parts clerk: Only the battery terminal, P1, is available. The cover, P2, awaits the supplier's response.
  3. Advisor: Then saying the parts are here would overstate the status of the whole job.
  4. Parts clerk: Correct. We need to identify each item rather than apply P1's status to both.
  5. Advisor: Is P2 confirmed as a back order, or is parts availability simply unconfirmed?
  6. Parts clerk: Availability is unconfirmed. We don't yet have evidence for the more specific back-order status.
  7. Advisor: I'll tell the customer one item is available and the second supply check remains pending.
  8. Parts clerk: That is accurate. Don't infer a completion date from the availability of only P1.
  9. Advisor: Please let the workshop know the same distinction so scheduling uses the actual supply status.
  10. Parts clerk: I'll send an item-specific update with P1 available and P2 awaiting the supplier's answer.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

REFERENCE
Course: Automotive Service English
Professional audience: Service reception staff, service advisors, automotive technicians, parts-counter staff, and workshop assistants
Scope: The vehicles, shop terms, charges, records, and events are fictional. This book teaches English, not vehicle diagnosis, repair, testing, driving decisions, or legal advice.
