Lesson 05: Explaining parts and completion delays
Original fictional case: The part required for job 315 is on back order. The supplier estimates arrival on Tuesday, but shipment is not confirmed. Customer Ben wants to collect the vehicle on Tuesday. Technician scheduling and completion are also unconfirmed. Parts clerk Nia promises a supply-status update on Monday afternoon. She must acknowledge the requested pickup without accepting it, preserve the supplier estimate as provisional, and avoid treating part arrival as a completed repair.
Language workshop: Tuesday arrival is not Tuesday collection
Communication goal: Explain a parts delay by separating supplier estimates, shipment confirmation, workshop scheduling, and a firm update commitment.
Language guidance: Tuesday is the supplier estimate for part arrival. Shipment is unconfirmed, and the part is on back order. Keep the estimate provisional rather than changing it to a guaranteed delivery or a booked collection.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The part for job 315 is on back order. / The supplier estimates arrival on Tuesday. / Shipment has not been confirmed. / A dispatch confirmation would not itself mean the part has arrived. / I understand that you would like Tuesday pickup. / I cannot confirm Tuesday collection from the current information. / Technician scheduling still needs confirmation. / Part arrival is not the same as completed work. / I will update you on supply status Monday afternoon. / That is a communication commitment, not a guaranteed supply result. / We have no confirmed allocation or shipment to report here. / Please keep job 315 attached to this parts update. / Tuesday remains estimated, not confirmed. / The completion time is still unconfirmed. / Back order, estimated Tuesday arrival, shipment and completion unconfirmed. / You will hear from me Monday afternoon about the supply position.
Vocabulary: back order: Order for an item not currently available for immediate supply. / supplier estimate: Provisional timing or quantity information given by the supplier. / estimated arrival: Expected delivery time not necessarily confirmed. / shipment confirmation: Evidence that goods have been dispatched under the stated arrangement. / dispatch: Sending goods from the supplier or distribution point. / delivery: Transfer of goods to the destination. / lead time: Time between an order stage and the relevant supply stage. / parts availability: Whether the required item can be supplied at the relevant time. / part allocation: Assignment of available stock to a particular order or job. / order status: Current position of a purchase or supply request. / supply-status update: Communication about progress or uncertainty in obtaining an item. / workshop scheduling: Assignment of technical work to available time and staff. / installation: Fitting the relevant component as part of authorized work. / completion confirmation: Verified statement that the relevant work has finished. / pickup request: Customer preference for collecting the vehicle at a stated time. / collection commitment: Agreed promise about when the vehicle can be handed over. / dependency: Prior condition on which a later stage relies. / provisional date: Date subject to confirmation or change. / confirmed date: Date established through the relevant process. / parts clerk: Staff member coordinating component identification and supply. / delay notice: Communication explaining a change or uncertainty in timing. / supplier follow-up: Further contact to clarify supply information. / progress checkpoint: Agreed point for reviewing and communicating status. / uncertain completion: Finish status or timing that has not been established.
Speaking task: Complete the parts-delay dialogue using the word bank. Read the exchanges about Tuesday and Monday afternoon aloud. Keep estimated arrival, supply update, and collection separate. Complete the transfer, then use the key to correct any phrase that turns an estimate into a confirmed commitment.
Partner: Ben: Customer / Nia: Parts clerk
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the delay update using the actual supplier estimate and Nia's communication commitment.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Ben: Read back the supplier estimate for me once. I'd been treating Tuesday as a promise.
Nia: The estimated arrival is Tuesday; shipment is unconfirmed. Technician scheduling and completion are also unconfirmed. I will update you about supply on Monday afternoon.
Ben: All right. I'll expect your Monday-afternoon update, with collection still unconfirmed.
Nia: Correct. We will keep completion confirmation separate from the parts forecast. You have a definite next update, but no invented guarantee about delivery, installation, or pickup.
END REFERENCE
06Relaying findings and technical termsA code is a clue, not a replacement order+

Your goal

Translate a diagnostic note into accurate customer language while separating recorded information, confirmed cause, and authorized work.

This lesson in the book, page 49 ↗

A Read the situation

A code is a clue, not a replacement order

Technician Jules has recorded a diagnostic trouble code, abbreviated DTC, on job 416. The note gives no confirmed cause or component fault. Customer Tomas assumes the code proves that a sensor needs replacement. Advisor Ava must explain the limited finding and request a clearer technician explanation. No replacement is approved. Ava has no additional test results and must not invent them or tell Tomas how to diagnose the vehicle.

Tomas: Customer / Ava: Service advisor

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does the note record?

Answer and explanation

C. A diagnostic trouble code

The technician note records a code without confirming the cause or component fault.

2. What has Tomas assumed?

Answer and explanation

D. The sensor must need replacement

Tomas has treated the code as proof of a specific replacement need.

3. What should Ava request next?

Answer and explanation

D. A clearer technician explanation

The missing explanation must come from the technician rather than the advisor's guess.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

DTC expands to diagnostic trouble code. It does not expand to a repair instruction. Job 416 contains a code, but the note does not identify a confirmed cause or component fault. Do not invent a code number or sensor type.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
diagnostic trouble code
Coded diagnostic information; abbreviated DTC.record a diagnostic trouble code
on-board diagnostics
Vehicle monitoring and reporting systems; abbreviated OBD.refer to on-board diagnostics
scan tool
Device for accessing supported diagnostic information.identify the scan-tool record
control module
Electronic unit managing specified vehicle functions.identify the control module
sensor
Component detecting a condition and supplying a signal.distinguish a sensor from its circuit
actuator
Component carrying out a commanded physical action.identify the actuator
circuit
Connected electrical path for a function.identify the relevant circuit
malfunction indicator lamp
Dashboard warning indicator; abbreviated MIL.report an illuminated lamp
stored code
Code retained in diagnostic memory.identify a stored code
pending code
Provisional code status, not a repair diagnosis.distinguish a pending code
freeze-frame data
Snapshot of operating information for a recorded event.interpret freeze-frame data
live data
Supported information displayed as conditions are read.distinguish live data
assessment note
Technician record of the examination and available findings.read the assessment note
confirmed cause
Explanation supported by the completed relevant assessment.distinguish a confirmed cause
component fault
Established problem attributed to a particular part.avoid inventing a component fault
diagnostic evidence
Information relevant to investigating a reported problem.explain the diagnostic evidence
fault tracing
Investigation intended to locate the source of a problem.distinguish fault tracing from code reading
replacement decision
Conclusion that a particular item should be replaced.support a replacement decision
technician clarification
Further explanation supplied by the relevant technician.request technician clarification
finding
Information established or recorded during an assessment.relay the limited finding
interpretation
Explanation of what information means in context.separate a record from its interpretation
repair authorization
Customer permission for a specified repair under the stated terms.obtain repair authorization
unsupported inference
Conclusion not established by the available information.correct an unsupported inference
plain-language explanation
Account using accessible words without changing the technical meaning.provide a plain-language explanation

Ava can relay the recorded finding and request clarification. The vocabulary introduces common diagnostic terms without claiming they all appear in this job. No replacement has been approved, and the conversation does not establish vehicle safety or readiness.

C Notice the language

Explain the technical noun and its evidential limit

Using the full term once helps a customer follow the conversation. The more important distinction is what the information establishes. Explain what is recorded, what has not been confirmed, and whose clarification is needed before discussing a replacement decision.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Expand the abbreviation
DTC stands for diagnostic trouble code.
Identify the source
I am reading the technician note for job 416.
State the finding
The note records a code.
Explain the limit
It does not confirm the cause.
Separate the component
It does not establish that this sensor has failed.
Correct the inference
A code is not automatically a replacement instruction.
Avoid invented detail
I do not have a specific test result to quote.
Preserve the uncertainty
The cause remains unconfirmed in this note.
Identify the missing explanation
We need the technician to explain what the finding supports.
Request clarification
Could you clarify whether a component fault has been established?
Use accessible language
The system recorded information that needs interpretation.
Distinguish authorization
No replacement has been approved.
Avoid a safety conclusion
This note does not give me a vehicle-readiness confirmation.
Check the understanding
Are we agreed that the code alone does not settle the repair?
State the next action
I will request a clearer explanation from Jules.
Close the handoff
I will keep the recorded code separate from any later repair proposal.
6 language and precision notes
Stands for
Introduces the full form of an abbreviation.
Does not establish
Limits a conclusion without denying that information was recorded.
Automatically
Identifies the mistaken leap from code to replacement.
In this note
Defines the information available to the speaker.
Has been approved
Refers to authorization, not simply a proposed action.
What the finding supports
Requests an evidence-based explanation rather than a more confident guess.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which explanation is accurate?

Answer and explanation

B. A code is recorded, but the cause is not confirmed

The available note records information without establishing a confirmed cause.

2. Which abbreviation is correctly expanded?

Answer and explanation

D. DTC: diagnostic trouble code

Diagnostic trouble code is the stated full term for DTC.

3. Which sentence invents evidence?

Answer and explanation

C. Jules tested the sensor and proved it faulty

No sensor-test result or confirmed component fault appears in the supplied facts.

4. Which handoff respects the approval limit?

Answer and explanation

B. Request clarification before any separate repair proposal

Clarification is needed, and no replacement authorization has been given.

E Conversations

A code is a clue, not a replacement order

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Tomas: Customer / Ava: Service advisor

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • component fault
  • interpretation
  • unsupported inference
  • finding
  • technician clarification
  • repair authorization
  • replacement decision
  • diagnostic trouble code
  • plain-language explanation
  • assessment note
  1. Tomas
    The message about 416 says there's a code. Does that mean you've found a bad sensor and ordered a replacement?
  2. Ava
    Not from this note. It records a 1, or DTC, but doesn't confirm the cause.
  3. Tomas
    I thought a code was basically the name of the part you needed. Otherwise, what is it telling us?
  4. Ava
    It can guide the investigation; it isn't automatically a 2. A recorded code isn't an instruction to order a sensor.
  5. Tomas
    Did Jules test that sensor, then? Perhaps there's another result missing from the message I received.
  6. Ava
    I can't see one here. The 3 records the code, with no supplied sensor-test result or confirmed component fault.
  7. Tomas
    That's the part I'm struggling with. I've got technical information, but I still don't know what you've actually established.
  8. Ava
    The recorded 4 is limited to the code. Its cause and whether a part needs replacing aren't established in this note.
  9. Tomas
    So a reference to a system or circuit wouldn't, by itself, prove the sensor is defective?
  10. Ava
    Correct. We haven't established a 5. I'm explaining the note's limit, not proposing a different diagnosis.
  11. Tomas
    Could you ask Jules to explain what the evidence does show? I need something more useful than just reading the code back.
  12. Ava
    I'll request 6 on what the assessment establishes and what remains unresolved before a repair proposal can be explained.
  13. Tomas
    Also, my question about a new sensor wasn't a request to buy one. Please don't treat it as an order.
  14. Ava
    Understood. No 7 has been given. I'll record your question as a request for explanation, not permission to order or fit a part.
  15. Tomas
    Can I collect the vehicle while that explanation is being sorted out? Or has nobody confirmed that either?
  16. Ava
    I have no readiness confirmation here. A 8 of a code mustn't become a claim that the vehicle is ready or safe.
  17. Tomas
    Tell Jules why I'm confused, please. I read code as sensor failure, and that's where the conversation went off track.
  18. Ava
    I'll include that. It's an 9 from the available note, and the clarification should address that specific misunderstanding.
  19. Tomas
    All right: code recorded, cause unconfirmed, no replacement approved. I need Jules's explanation, not a parts order.
  20. Ava
    I'll request that 10 against job 416. Any later repair proposal remains separate from this clarification and will need its own approval.

Answers and explanations
  1. diagnostic trouble code Turn 2. Diagnostic trouble code is the recorded information, not proof that a particular sensor needs replacement.
  2. replacement decision Turn 4. Replacement decision requires support beyond treating the recorded code as a parts-order instruction.
  3. assessment note Turn 6. Assessment note is the available technician record, which contains no supplied sensor-test result.
  4. finding Turn 8. Finding here is limited to the recorded code, not a completed diagnosis or readiness conclusion.
  5. component fault Turn 10. Component fault would attribute an established problem to a particular part, which this note does not do.
  6. technician clarification Turn 12. Technician clarification asks Jules to explain the available evidence rather than having Ava supply a guess.
  7. repair authorization Turn 14. Repair authorization has not been given merely because Tomas asked whether a sensor needs replacement.
  8. plain-language explanation Turn 16. Plain-language explanation makes the note understandable without adding a vehicle-readiness or safety claim.
  9. unsupported inference Turn 18. Unsupported inference identifies the leap from a code to proven sensor failure without dismissing the customer's question.
  10. interpretation Turn 20. Interpretation explains the significance and limits of the information without inventing a diagnosis or approval.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Tomas: The message about 416 says there's a code. Does that mean you've found a bad sensor and ordered a replacement?
  2. Ava: Not from this note. It records a diagnostic trouble code, or DTC, but doesn't confirm the cause.
  3. Tomas: I thought a code was basically the name of the part you needed. Otherwise, what is it telling us?
  4. Ava: It can guide the investigation; it isn't automatically a replacement decision. A recorded code isn't an instruction to order a sensor.
  5. Tomas: Did Jules test that sensor, then? Perhaps there's another result missing from the message I received.
  6. Ava: I can't see one here. The assessment note records the code, with no supplied sensor-test result or confirmed component fault.
  7. Tomas: That's the part I'm struggling with. I've got technical information, but I still don't know what you've actually established.
  8. Ava: The recorded finding is limited to the code. Its cause and whether a part needs replacing aren't established in this note.
  9. Tomas: So a reference to a system or circuit wouldn't, by itself, prove the sensor is defective?
  10. Ava: Correct. We haven't established a component fault. I'm explaining the note's limit, not proposing a different diagnosis.
  11. Tomas: Could you ask Jules to explain what the evidence does show? I need something more useful than just reading the code back.
  12. Ava: I'll request technician clarification on what the assessment establishes and what remains unresolved before a repair proposal can be explained.
  13. Tomas: Also, my question about a new sensor wasn't a request to buy one. Please don't treat it as an order.
  14. Ava: Understood. No repair authorization has been given. I'll record your question as a request for explanation, not permission to order or fit a part.
  15. Tomas: Can I collect the vehicle while that explanation is being sorted out? Or has nobody confirmed that either?
  16. Ava: I have no readiness confirmation here. A plain-language explanation of a code mustn't become a claim that the vehicle is ready or safe.
  17. Tomas: Tell Jules why I'm confused, please. I read code as sensor failure, and that's where the conversation went off track.
  18. Ava: I'll include that. It's an unsupported inference from the available note, and the clarification should address that specific misunderstanding.
  19. Tomas: All right: code recorded, cause unconfirmed, no replacement approved. I need Jules's explanation, not a parts order.
  20. Ava: I'll request that interpretation against job 416. Any later repair proposal remains separate from this clarification and will need its own approval.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Not reproduced is a limited finding

An advisor explains a technician's assessment outcome.

  1. Customer: The report says not reproduced. Are you saying I imagined the rattle?
  2. Advisor: No. The technician didn't reproduce it during this assessment; that doesn't invalidate your report.
  3. Customer: So you haven't confirmed that nothing is wrong under every possible condition?
  4. Advisor: Correct. The cause remains unconfirmed, and I shouldn't turn this finding into a broader guarantee.
  5. Customer: I'd like someone to explain what the finding means before we discuss further options.
  6. Advisor: I can arrange a technician explanation and preserve your question on the repair order.
  7. Customer: Please don't record my request as approval for another assessment or a repair.
  8. Advisor: I won't. Asking for an explanation is separate from deciding on additional work.
  9. Customer: And can reception tell me whether the car is safe to drive now?
  10. Advisor: That question needs the qualified technician's response; I can't provide a safety assurance myself.
A recommendation needs an explanation

A customer asks an advisor about a technical recommendation.

  1. Customer: The report recommends further assessment, but the brief message doesn't explain the reason.
  2. Advisor: I can ask the technician to explain the finding behind that recommendation in plain language.
  3. Customer: Please separate what they observed from what they are proposing to do next.
  4. Advisor: I will. An inspection finding and a recommendation serve different purposes in the report.
  5. Customer: Does the recommendation mean additional work has already been approved on my behalf?
  6. Advisor: No. It remains a proposal for discussion, with scope and terms to be clarified.
  7. Customer: I want to understand those details before deciding, not reject everything automatically.
  8. Advisor: I'll preserve that position: explanation requested, decision pending, no new authorization assumed.
  9. Customer: Could the technician also clarify any unfamiliar term rather than just repeat it louder?
  10. Advisor: Yes. I'll ask for an explanation tied to your actual finding and proposed options.
A stored code is not a failed component

An advisor clarifies the limits of a technician's recorded diagnostic trouble code.

  1. Customer: The report shows a diagnostic trouble code. Does that prove a sensor failed?
  2. Advisor: No. The technician recorded the code, but hasn't confirmed its underlying cause.
  3. Customer: I thought a DTC named the exact part that definitely needed replacing.
  4. Advisor: A diagnostic trouble code indicates a monitored condition for investigation, not automatic component failure.
  5. Customer: Then please don't describe my question as permission to replace a sensor.
  6. Advisor: I won't. No replacement is approved, and the technician needs to explain this finding.
  7. Customer: Can they distinguish the stored code from anything actually reproduced during the assessment?
  8. Advisor: I'll ask for that distinction and any proposed next step, with its scope explained.
  9. Customer: Please link the explanation to my current report rather than a generic list of codes.
  10. Advisor: I will. Your code finding and any later diagnosis will remain separately identified.

Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Lesson 06: Relaying findings and technical terms
Original fictional case: Technician Jules has recorded a diagnostic trouble code, abbreviated DTC, on job 416. The note gives no confirmed cause or component fault. Customer Tomas assumes the code proves that a sensor needs replacement. Advisor Ava must explain the limited finding and request a clearer technician explanation. No replacement is approved. Ava has no additional test results and must not invent them or tell Tomas how to diagnose the vehicle.
Language workshop: A code is a clue, not a replacement order
Communication goal: Translate a diagnostic note into accurate customer language while separating recorded information, confirmed cause, and authorized work.
Language guidance: DTC expands to diagnostic trouble code. It does not expand to a repair instruction. Job 416 contains a code, but the note does not identify a confirmed cause or component fault. Do not invent a code number or sensor type.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): DTC stands for diagnostic trouble code. / I am reading the technician note for job 416. / The note records a code. / It does not confirm the cause. / It does not establish that this sensor has failed. / A code is not automatically a replacement instruction. / I do not have a specific test result to quote. / The cause remains unconfirmed in this note. / We need the technician to explain what the finding supports. / Could you clarify whether a component fault has been established? / The system recorded information that needs interpretation. / No replacement has been approved. / This note does not give me a vehicle-readiness confirmation. / Are we agreed that the code alone does not settle the repair? / I will request a clearer explanation from Jules. / I will keep the recorded code separate from any later repair proposal.
Vocabulary: diagnostic trouble code: Coded diagnostic information; abbreviated DTC. / on-board diagnostics: Vehicle monitoring and reporting systems; abbreviated OBD. / scan tool: Device for accessing supported diagnostic information. / control module: Electronic unit managing specified vehicle functions. / sensor: Component detecting a condition and supplying a signal. / actuator: Component carrying out a commanded physical action. / circuit: Connected electrical path for a function. / malfunction indicator lamp: Dashboard warning indicator; abbreviated MIL. / stored code: Code retained in diagnostic memory. / pending code: Provisional code status, not a repair diagnosis. / freeze-frame data: Snapshot of operating information for a recorded event. / live data: Supported information displayed as conditions are read. / assessment note: Technician record of the examination and available findings. / confirmed cause: Explanation supported by the completed relevant assessment. / component fault: Established problem attributed to a particular part. / diagnostic evidence: Information relevant to investigating a reported problem. / fault tracing: Investigation intended to locate the source of a problem. / replacement decision: Conclusion that a particular item should be replaced. / technician clarification: Further explanation supplied by the relevant technician. / finding: Information established or recorded during an assessment. / interpretation: Explanation of what information means in context. / repair authorization: Customer permission for a specified repair under the stated terms. / unsupported inference: Conclusion not established by the available information. / plain-language explanation: Account using accessible words without changing the technical meaning.
Speaking task: Complete the dialogue about the recorded code. Read Tomas's misunderstanding and Ava's correction aloud. Keep code, confirmed cause, repair approval, and readiness distinct. Complete the transfer and check each explanation. Reread the corrected explanation without naming an unsupported failed part.
Partner: Tomas: Customer / Ava: Service advisor
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the advisor readback. Use the recorded evidence and approval status only.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Tomas: Tell Jules why I'm confused, please. I read code as sensor failure, and that's where the conversation went off track.
Ava: I'll include that. It's an unsupported inference from the available note, and the clarification should address that specific misunderstanding.
Tomas: All right: code recorded, cause unconfirmed, no replacement approved. I need Jules's explanation, not a parts order.
Ava: I'll request that interpretation against job 416. Any later repair proposal remains separate from this clarification and will need its own approval.
END REFERENCE

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

07Clarifying the invoice and collection handoffThe bill is ready; the vehicle status is not+

Your goal

Explain a two-line invoice accurately and separate a financial document from verified collection readiness.

This lesson in the book, page 58 ↗

A Read the situation

The bill is ready; the vehicle status is not

The fictional invoice for job 527 totals $180: $70 for parts and $110 for labor. Customer Imani thinks the labor line includes another $70 parts charge. Advisor Leo must explain that the two listed charges add to $180, not $250. The service desk has not yet confirmed vehicle readiness. Issuing an invoice does not itself confirm collection. No additional fees, taxes, payment status, or release arrangements are supplied.

Imani: Customer / Leo: Service advisor

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is the stated invoice total?

Answer and explanation

A. $180

The supplied invoice contains $70 parts plus $110 labor, totaling $180.

2. What has Imani misunderstood?

Answer and explanation

B. She thinks another $70 parts charge is included in labor

Her concern is an apparent extra parts charge within the labor line.

3. What remains unconfirmed?

Answer and explanation

A. Vehicle readiness

The service desk has not yet supplied vehicle-readiness confirmation.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The arithmetic is $70 + $110 = $180. The labor line is not $110 plus an additional $70 parts charge. The supplied invoice has no third charge. Explain the stated figures without introducing a tax rate, discount, or payment.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
invoice
Document listing charges for supplied work or items.explain the invoice
line item
Separately listed entry on a bill or estimate.identify each line item
parts charge
Amount billed for the listed vehicle components or materials.distinguish the parts charge
labor charge
Amount billed for the stated work rather than the parts.explain the labor charge
invoice total
Combined amount shown as payable on the invoice.confirm the invoice total
duplicate charge
Amount billed again for the same chargeable item.investigate a suspected duplicate charge
itemized bill
Bill that separates charges into identifiable entries.read the itemized bill
billing query
Customer request for clarification about a charge.resolve a billing query
arithmetic
Calculation of the amounts involved.check the arithmetic
subtotal
Amount before any separately applicable later additions or deductions.distinguish a subtotal from the stated total
tax line
Separate entry identifying an applicable tax charge.avoid inventing a tax line
discount
Reduction from an otherwise stated price.identify an authorized discount
credit adjustment
Recorded reduction or correction applied to an account.distinguish a credit adjustment
balance due
Amount still payable after relevant recorded payments or credits.verify the balance due
payment status
Record of whether and how an amount has been paid.check payment status
receipt
Record acknowledging a payment or transaction.distinguish a receipt from an unpaid invoice
invoice date
Date assigned to the issued billing document.verify the invoice date
job reference
Identifier linking the bill and service record.match the job reference
collection readiness
Confirmed status for the relevant vehicle handover arrangements.verify collection readiness
service desk
Team or location coordinating service information and handover.check with the service desk
handover confirmation
Verified information that the agreed transfer can proceed.obtain handover confirmation
release arrangements
Agreed details governing the transfer of the vehicle.confirm release arrangements
billing record
Account of charges and related financial entries.correct the billing record
readiness check
Request to verify the vehicle's current handover status.request a readiness check

An invoice and a readiness confirmation serve different purposes. The invoice explains charges; the service desk must confirm collection status. Do not infer that the customer has paid, the vehicle is ready, or a particular release requirement has been met.

C Notice the language

Explain the arithmetic before defending the bill

A customer can question a charge without accusing anyone of dishonesty. Identify the two lines and show how they form the stated total. Then address collection separately, so a successful billing explanation does not become an accidental readiness promise.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Invite the query
Which line would you like me to explain?
Identify the reference
We are looking at job 527.
Separate the entries
There are two listed charges.
Name the first amount
The parts charge is $70.
Name the second amount
The labor charge is $110.
State the calculation
Seventy plus one hundred ten equals one hundred eighty.
Correct the duplicate assumption
There is no additional $70 parts charge in that labor line.
Acknowledge the confusion
I can see why the layout prompted that question.
Keep to the supplied bill
I will not add a charge that is not listed.
Avoid assuming payment
The invoice alone does not establish payment status.
Separate documents
A bill and a receipt are not the same record.
Switch to collection
Let us check readiness separately from the invoice.
State the missing status
The service desk has not confirmed readiness yet.
Avoid a false invitation
I cannot ask you to collect on the basis of this document alone.
Request the handoff check
Please confirm the collection position for job 527.
Close accurately
The invoice total is clear; collection remains unconfirmed.
6 language and precision notes
Plus versus included in
Distinguishes adding two lines from imagining another charge inside one line.
There are two
Limits the explanation to the supplied invoice entries.
Prompted that question
Acknowledges confusion without accepting an incorrect calculation.
Alone
Identifies the limit of what a single document establishes.
Yet
States the current absence of confirmation without promising when it will arrive.
Separately from
Keeps financial clarification and collection status distinct.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which explanation resolves the billing query?

Answer and explanation

A. $70 parts plus $110 labor gives the $180 total

The supplied bill contains two charges whose sum is exactly $180.

2. Which statement wrongly infers readiness?

Answer and explanation

A. The invoice exists, so come and collect now

Invoice creation does not establish the vehicle's collection status.

3. What can Leo say about payment?

Answer and explanation

A. The supplied facts do not establish payment status

No payment or receipt information is included in the supplied facts.

4. Which request should Leo send?

Answer and explanation

C. Confirm collection status for job 527

The service desk must verify readiness independently of the invoice explanation.

E Conversations

The bill is ready; the vehicle status is not

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Imani: Customer / Leo: Service advisor

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • job reference
  • billing query
  • billing record
  • invoice total
  • collection readiness
  • payment status
  • itemized bill
  • handover confirmation
  • parts charge
  • readiness check
  1. Imani
    The invoice for 527 says seventy for parts and a hundred ten for labor. I added seventy to the total and got two hundred fifty.
  2. Leo
    No. There are two separate entries on this 1. The parts charge is seventy dollars and the labor charge is one hundred ten dollars.
  3. Imani
    Then the parts are already in the hundred eighty. I shouldn't add them again.
  4. Leo
    Correct. The 2 is one hundred eighty dollars: seventy plus one hundred ten. There is no extra seventy-dollar parts charge inside the labor line.
  5. Imani
    Could you point out the total separately from the two charges? That's where I went wrong.
  6. Leo
    I understand the 3. The layout led you to ask whether parts were being charged twice. Here, the two lines represent parts and labor separately.
  7. Imani
    I'm asking about the calculation, not a discount. The same parts charge should appear only once in the sum.
  8. Leo
    Certainly. The 4 is seventy dollars. Labor is one hundred ten dollars. Those two figures produce the stated total of one hundred eighty dollars.
  9. Imani
    Does having the invoice mean I can come and collect the vehicle now?
  10. Leo
    Please let me check 5 separately. The service desk has not confirmed it yet, so I should not invite you to collect on the strength of the invoice.
  11. Imani
    Please leave the itemized charges as they are. It was my addition that needed correcting.
  12. Leo
    Yes. The 6 tells us about the charges. It does not, by itself, give the missing handover confirmation or settle when you should travel here.
  13. Imani
    Also, I haven't told you I've paid. An invoice total isn't a payment confirmation.
  14. Leo
    We have not established 7 in this conversation. An invoice is not automatically a receipt, and I will not say a payment has been made without the relevant record.
  15. Imani
    Could you check readiness separately before I make the trip?
  16. Leo
    Exactly. We have clarified the arithmetic. The next 8 should go to the service desk for job 527, without treating the bill as proof of readiness.
  17. Imani
    Use job 527 for that check, please. I want the readiness message linked to the same vehicle record.
  18. Leo
    I will match the 9 before requesting confirmation. The figures we discussed remain seventy for parts and one hundred ten for labor, totaling one hundred eighty.
  19. Imani
    So the bill is seventy plus a hundred ten, total a hundred eighty; collection still needs confirmation.
  20. Leo
    Understood. We still need 10. I will keep the billing explanation and the collection inquiry clear, so resolving one does not accidentally promise the other.

Answers and explanations
  1. itemized bill Turn 2. Itemized bill separates the parts and labor entries so their amounts can be explained individually.
  2. invoice total Turn 4. Invoice total is $180 because the two stated charges are $70 and $110.
  3. billing query Turn 6. Billing query names Imani's request to understand the apparent extra charge without assuming an accusation.
  4. parts charge Turn 8. Parts charge is the $70 entry, distinct from the $110 labor entry.
  5. collection readiness Turn 10. Collection readiness has not been confirmed merely because the invoice was issued.
  6. billing record Turn 12. Billing record explains the charges but is not the missing vehicle-handover confirmation.
  7. payment status Turn 14. Payment status is not supplied, so Leo must not infer that the invoice has been paid.
  8. readiness check Turn 16. Readiness check asks the service desk to verify the unresolved collection position.
  9. job reference Turn 18. Job reference connects the collection inquiry and billing conversation to the same service job.
  10. handover confirmation Turn 20. Handover confirmation is the missing readiness information needed before collection arrangements are treated as settled.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Imani: The invoice for 527 says seventy for parts and a hundred ten for labor. I added seventy to the total and got two hundred fifty.
  2. Leo: No. There are two separate entries on this itemized bill. The parts charge is seventy dollars and the labor charge is one hundred ten dollars.
  3. Imani: Then the parts are already in the hundred eighty. I shouldn't add them again.
  4. Leo: Correct. The invoice total is one hundred eighty dollars: seventy plus one hundred ten. There is no extra seventy-dollar parts charge inside the labor line.
  5. Imani: Could you point out the total separately from the two charges? That's where I went wrong.
  6. Leo: I understand the billing query. The layout led you to ask whether parts were being charged twice. Here, the two lines represent parts and labor separately.
  7. Imani: I'm asking about the calculation, not a discount. The same parts charge should appear only once in the sum.
  8. Leo: Certainly. The parts charge is seventy dollars. Labor is one hundred ten dollars. Those two figures produce the stated total of one hundred eighty dollars.
  9. Imani: Does having the invoice mean I can come and collect the vehicle now?
  10. Leo: Please let me check collection readiness separately. The service desk has not confirmed it yet, so I should not invite you to collect on the strength of the invoice.
  11. Imani: Please leave the itemized charges as they are. It was my addition that needed correcting.
  12. Leo: Yes. The billing record tells us about the charges. It does not, by itself, give the missing handover confirmation or settle when you should travel here.
  13. Imani: Also, I haven't told you I've paid. An invoice total isn't a payment confirmation.
  14. Leo: We have not established payment status in this conversation. An invoice is not automatically a receipt, and I will not say a payment has been made without the relevant record.
  15. Imani: Could you check readiness separately before I make the trip?
  16. Leo: Exactly. We have clarified the arithmetic. The next readiness check should go to the service desk for job 527, without treating the bill as proof of readiness.
  17. Imani: Use job 527 for that check, please. I want the readiness message linked to the same vehicle record.
  18. Leo: I will match the job reference before requesting confirmation. The figures we discussed remain seventy for parts and one hundred ten for labor, totaling one hundred eighty.
  19. Imani: So the bill is seventy plus a hundred ten, total a hundred eighty; collection still needs confirmation.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Parts and labor are separate lines

An advisor explains a fictional $180 invoice.

  1. Customer: The invoice lists seventy for parts and one hundred ten for labor. Is seventy charged twice?
  2. Advisor: No. The seventy-dollar parts line and one-hundred-ten-dollar labor charge total one hundred eighty.
  3. Customer: Thank you. I read the headings too quickly and combined them incorrectly.
  4. Advisor: I can walk through the itemized invoice using the actual descriptions shown here.
  5. Customer: Since the invoice is available, can I come and collect the vehicle immediately?
  6. Advisor: Not yet on that basis. The service desk still needs to confirm collection readiness.
  7. Customer: So billing information and the ready-to-collect message are separate confirmations in this case?
  8. Advisor: Exactly. I'll ask for the actual vehicle status rather than infer it from the bill.
  9. Customer: Please call me when that status is confirmed, so I can arrange my ride.
  10. Advisor: I'll record your call preference and keep the collection question open until the desk responds.
A friend offers to collect

A customer asks reception about a different collector.

  1. Customer: My friend can collect the car. Is giving them a copy of the invoice enough?
  2. Receptionist: The invoice alone doesn't establish an authorized collector under this shop's collection requirements.
  3. Customer: Then please explain what the service desk needs to confirm my collection arrangement.
  4. Receptionist: I'll connect you with them so the identity and permission details are checked appropriately.
  5. Customer: Please don't change the owner details on the account; I'm only asking about collection.
  6. Receptionist: Understood. Collector permission and the vehicle's account information are different parts of the record.
  7. Customer: Has the vehicle already been confirmed ready, or are we arranging collection in advance?
  8. Receptionist: The ready-to-collect confirmation is still pending. Your collector question doesn't change that status.
  9. Customer: Then my friend should wait for both the arrangement and readiness confirmation before traveling.
  10. Receptionist: Correct. I'll pass both outstanding questions to the desk without treating either as settled.
A receipt and an invoice are different records

A customer clarifies two service documents with an advisor.

  1. Customer: This document says invoice. Does that prove the payment has already been received?
  2. Advisor: Not by its title alone. Let me check the payment status separately from the listed charges.
  3. Customer: I don't want to make a second payment because I misunderstood which document I received.
  4. Advisor: The account shows payment received, and the receipt reference is linked to this invoice.
  5. Customer: Then please identify that receipt so I can keep the records together.
  6. Advisor: Here is the matching reference. It confirms the recorded payment rather than adding another charge.
  7. Customer: Does that payment record also confirm every service item I asked about was performed?
  8. Advisor: No. Work performed is described on the completed job record; payment doesn't expand its scope.
  9. Customer: I'll compare those records rather than assume all my earlier questions became completed work.
  10. Advisor: I can help identify the relevant lines and refer any technical completion question to the technician.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

  1. Complete the invoice dialogue.
  2. Read the arithmetic aloud: seventy for parts plus one hundred ten for labor equals one hundred eighty. Then read the separate readiness exchange.

Answer and explanation

C. seventy

Seventy is the supplied parts entry, not an additional charge within labor.

Answer and explanation

B. ten

Ten completes the stated labor amount of one hundred ten dollars.

3. Advisor: "The two charges total one hundred ___ dollars."

Answer and explanation

B. eighty

Eighty completes the correct sum of seventy and one hundred ten dollars.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.

4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Lesson 07: Clarifying the invoice and collection handoff
Original fictional case: The fictional invoice for job 527 totals $180: $70 for parts and $110 for labor. Customer Imani thinks the labor line includes another $70 parts charge. Advisor Leo must explain that the two listed charges add to $180, not $250. The service desk has not yet confirmed vehicle readiness. Issuing an invoice does not itself confirm collection. No additional fees, taxes, payment status, or release arrangements are supplied.
Language workshop: The bill is ready; the vehicle status is not
Communication goal: Explain a two-line invoice accurately and separate a financial document from verified collection readiness.
Language guidance: The arithmetic is $70 + $110 = $180. The labor line is not $110 plus an additional $70 parts charge. The supplied invoice has no third charge. Explain the stated figures without introducing a tax rate, discount, or payment.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Which line would you like me to explain? / We are looking at job 527. / There are two listed charges. / The parts charge is $70. / The labor charge is $110. / Seventy plus one hundred ten equals one hundred eighty. / There is no additional $70 parts charge in that labor line. / I can see why the layout prompted that question. / I will not add a charge that is not listed. / The invoice alone does not establish payment status. / A bill and a receipt are not the same record. / Let us check readiness separately from the invoice. / The service desk has not confirmed readiness yet. / I cannot ask you to collect on the basis of this document alone. / Please confirm the collection position for job 527. / The invoice total is clear; collection remains unconfirmed.
Vocabulary: invoice: Document listing charges for supplied work or items. / line item: Separately listed entry on a bill or estimate. / parts charge: Amount billed for the listed vehicle components or materials. / labor charge: Amount billed for the stated work rather than the parts. / invoice total: Combined amount shown as payable on the invoice. / duplicate charge: Amount billed again for the same chargeable item. / itemized bill: Bill that separates charges into identifiable entries. / billing query: Customer request for clarification about a charge. / arithmetic: Calculation of the amounts involved. / subtotal: Amount before any separately applicable later additions or deductions. / tax line: Separate entry identifying an applicable tax charge. / discount: Reduction from an otherwise stated price. / credit adjustment: Recorded reduction or correction applied to an account. / balance due: Amount still payable after relevant recorded payments or credits. / payment status: Record of whether and how an amount has been paid. / receipt: Record acknowledging a payment or transaction. / invoice date: Date assigned to the issued billing document. / job reference: Identifier linking the bill and service record. / collection readiness: Confirmed status for the relevant vehicle handover arrangements. / service desk: Team or location coordinating service information and handover. / handover confirmation: Verified information that the agreed transfer can proceed. / release arrangements: Agreed details governing the transfer of the vehicle. / billing record: Account of charges and related financial entries. / readiness check: Request to verify the vehicle's current handover status.
Speaking task: Complete the invoice dialogue. Read the arithmetic aloud: seventy for parts plus one hundred ten for labor equals one hundred eighty. Then read the separate readiness exchange. Complete the transfer and check the key. Correct any answer that counts the parts twice or treats an invoice as a collection confirmation.
Partner: Imani: Customer / Leo: Service advisor
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the customer-service exchange using the two charges and the unresolved collection status.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Imani: Use job 527 for that check, please. I want the readiness message linked to the same vehicle record.
Leo: I will match the job reference before requesting confirmation. The figures we discussed remain seventy for parts and one hundred ten for labor, totaling one hundred eighty.
Imani: So the bill is seventy plus a hundred ten, total a hundred eighty; collection still needs confirmation.
Leo: Understood. We still need handover confirmation. I will keep the billing explanation and the collection inquiry clear, so resolving one does not accidentally promise the other.
END REFERENCE

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

08Handling a repeat concern and follow-upCorrect the dismissal before arranging the callback+

Your goal

Acknowledge a repeat report, correct an unsupported dismissal, and arrange a specific review without promising a diagnosis or coverage.

This lesson in the book, page 67 ↗

A Read the situation

Correct the dismissal before arranging the callback

Two days after service, customer Farah reports the same intermittent rattle. Advisor Daniel initially said, "That cannot be the same issue." The cause and warranty coverage are unknown. Service lead Nia can call at 15:00 to review the report and next arrangements. Daniel must withdraw the unsupported statement, record what Farah reports, and explain the purpose of the callback. The call is not a repair appointment or a promise of free work.

Farah: Customer / Daniel: Service advisor

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does Farah report?

Answer and explanation

D. The same intermittent rattle two days after service

The brief gives a repeat customer report, not a technical conclusion about its cause.

2. What should Daniel correct?

Answer and explanation

D. His claim that it cannot be the same issue

The initial dismissal was unsupported because the cause remains unknown.

3. What can Nia do at 15:00?

Answer and explanation

B. Call to review the report and next arrangements

The stated commitment is a review call rather than a repair or coverage guarantee.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Farah reports the same rattle, not a proven identical cause. Daniel should correct his statement that the issue cannot be the same. He should not replace that dismissal with a claim that the earlier repair definitely failed.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
repeat concern
Problem reported again after an earlier visit or intervention.acknowledge a repeat concern
return complaint
Customer report that brings an unresolved or recurring concern back to the business.record the return complaint
service history
Record of previous vehicle visits and relevant work.review the service history
repair record
Account of the specified work performed during a service visit.consult the repair record
reported recurrence
Customer account that a symptom has happened again.preserve the reported recurrence
intermittent symptom
Experienced condition that occurs at intervals.describe an intermittent symptom
same symptom
Similar experienced behavior or sound, without proving the same cause.distinguish the same symptom from the same cause
underlying cause
Explanation responsible for the reported condition.avoid assuming the underlying cause
unsupported dismissal
Rejection of a report without adequate evidence.withdraw an unsupported dismissal
acknowledgment
Clear recognition that the report has been heard and recorded.give a specific acknowledgment
apology
Statement accepting responsibility for an inappropriate response.make a direct apology
correction
Explicit replacement of an inaccurate statement.put the correction on record
service lead
Person responsible for coordinating the relevant service review.refer the report to the service lead
callback
Return telephone contact arranged for a stated purpose.confirm the callback
review appointment
Arranged discussion or assessment, defined by its actual scope.clarify the review appointment
next arrangements
Further practical steps to be agreed after the review.discuss next arrangements
warranty coverage
Protection that depends on the applicable terms and relevant facts.check warranty coverage
service contract
Separate agreement describing specified services or protection.distinguish a service contract from a warranty
coverage determination
Decision about whether stated protection applies.avoid promising a coverage determination
goodwill offer
Discretionary concession made by a business.avoid inventing a goodwill offer
no-charge work
Work supplied without a stated customer charge.distinguish no-charge work from a review call
follow-up record
Account of contacts and steps after the original service.update the follow-up record
complaint ownership
Responsibility for handling the stated concern or communication.clarify complaint ownership
communication repair
Correction of a mishandled exchange through acknowledgment and accurate follow-up.begin communication repair

Nia can call at 15:00 to review the report and next arrangements. That is not an appointment for a completed repair. Warranty coverage and cause remain unknown, and Daniel has no authority in the supplied facts to promise no-charge work.

C Notice the language

Correct your own statement explicitly

A calm apology is stronger when it identifies what was wrong. Withdraw the unsupported dismissal without swinging to an equally unsupported promise. Preserve the customer report, distinguish it from a confirmed cause, and make the next contact useful and specific.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the return
You are reporting the same intermittent rattle.
Preserve the timing
You noticed it again two days after service.
Own the earlier wording
I said it could not be the same issue.
Withdraw the claim
I should not have made that statement.
Apologize directly
I am sorry I dismissed your report.
State the uncertainty
We have not established the cause.
Keep symptoms and causes separate
A similar sound does not by itself prove an identical cause.
Avoid reversing into certainty
I cannot say the previous work definitely failed either.
Preserve the history
I will keep this report with the earlier service record.
Name the contact
Nia is the service lead handling the review call.
Specify the time
Nia can call at 15:00.
Explain the purpose
The call is to review your report and next arrangements.
Limit the commitment
This is not a confirmed repair appointment.
Keep coverage open
Warranty coverage has not been determined.
Avoid an unauthorized concession
I cannot promise no-charge work from the information available.
Close with a readback
Your report is recorded, my earlier dismissal is corrected, and the review call is at 15:00.
6 language and precision notes
I should not have
Accepts responsibility for a specific past statement.
Reporting the same
Attributes similarity to the customer's experience.
By itself
Limits what one observation can prove.
Either
Prevents the correction from becoming an opposite unsupported claim.
Can call at
Specifies an available contact commitment rather than repair completion.
Has not been determined
Keeps coverage unresolved without denying that it might apply.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which apology is most appropriate?

Answer and explanation

A. I am sorry I dismissed your report; the cause has not been established

The response owns the unsupported dismissal without inventing a diagnosis or remedy.

2. Which note preserves the evidence?

Answer and explanation

B. Customer reports same intermittent rattle two days after service

The note retains timing and attribution without adding an unestablished technical conclusion.

3. What does 15:00 refer to?

Answer and explanation

B. Nia's review call

The brief identifies 15:00 as the time of the service lead's call.

4. Which response answers the coverage question accurately?

Answer and explanation

D. Coverage needs review; the callback does not decide it

Neither the cause nor coverage has been determined; a callback and an apology do not establish either.

Check explanations in the book, page 99 ↗

E Conversations

Correct the dismissal before arranging the callback

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Farah: Customer / Daniel: Service advisor

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

  1. Farah
    The same rattle is back two days after the service. When you said that couldn't be the same issue, it sounded like you weren't listening.
  2. Daniel
    I'm sorry. That was an 1. I had no basis to say it couldn't be the same issue.
  3. Farah
    I can't tell you which part is involved. I just recognize the same intermittent sound, and I want that taken seriously.
  4. Daniel
    I'll record a 2 of the same intermittent rattle, two days after service. The cause isn't established yet.
  5. Farah
    Will Nia see the earlier visit? I don't want to start again as though nobody has worked on the vehicle.
  6. Daniel
    Yes. I'll connect this report to the 3 so she can review it alongside the previous record.
  7. Farah
    Does that mean you're accepting that the repair failed? I need to understand what your apology actually covers.
  8. Daniel
    I'm correcting my response. The 4 is still unknown, so I can't say the earlier work failed or that this concern is unrelated.
  9. Farah
    Fair enough. Who is actually going to follow this up, and when will I hear from them?
  10. Daniel
    Nia, our 5, can call at 15:00 to review your report and discuss the next arrangements.
  11. Farah
    Three this afternoon, by phone? I don't need to bring the car in at three?
  12. Daniel
    Right, it's a 6 at three. It isn't a repair booking, a collection time, or a promise of completion.
  13. Farah
    I'll be available. But I do need to ask about cost: the service was only two days ago.
  14. Daniel
    I understand. 7 hasn't been determined. I can't promise free work or rule coverage out from what we have.
  15. Farah
    Please pass that question on too. I don't want the call to end with me explaining the cost concern all over again.
  16. Daniel
    I'll put it in the 8 with your report and my correction. There won't be a coverage decision recorded before one is made.
  17. Farah
    Could you read back the arrangement, so I know exactly what Nia will receive?
  18. Daniel
    Your 9 is linked to the earlier visit: same intermittent rattle, two days later. My dismissal is withdrawn; Nia can call at 15:00 to review it.
  19. Farah
    Yes. I understand the call doesn't settle the cause or the cost, but at least the earlier report won't be lost.
  20. Daniel
    That's the start of the 10. I'll pass on the complete history and coverage question, with the agreed call time and no invented outcome.

Answers and explanations
  1. unsupported dismissal Turn 2. Unsupported dismissal describes Daniel's rejection of the report without an established cause.
  2. reported recurrence Turn 4. Reported recurrence preserves Farah's account without turning it into a confirmed technical diagnosis.
  3. service history Turn 6. Service history keeps the earlier visit available during review of the repeat report.
  4. underlying cause Turn 8. Underlying cause remains unknown even after Daniel corrects his inappropriate response.
  5. service lead Turn 10. Service lead identifies the named person who can review the report and discuss next arrangements.
  6. callback Turn 12. Callback is the promised telephone review, not a vehicle drop-off or repair appointment.
  7. Warranty coverage Turn 14. Warranty coverage is unresolved and must not be promised or denied without the relevant review.
  8. follow-up record Turn 16. Follow-up record carries the repeat concern, earlier correction, and coverage question into the next conversation.
  9. repeat concern Turn 18. Repeat concern is the same intermittent rattle reported again two days after the earlier service.
  10. communication repair Turn 20. Communication repair corrects the mishandled response while keeping diagnosis, coverage, and later arrangements unresolved.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Farah: The same rattle is back two days after the service. When you said that couldn't be the same issue, it sounded like you weren't listening.
  2. Daniel: I'm sorry. That was an unsupported dismissal. I had no basis to say it couldn't be the same issue.
  3. Farah: I can't tell you which part is involved. I just recognize the same intermittent sound, and I want that taken seriously.
  4. Daniel: I'll record a reported recurrence of the same intermittent rattle, two days after service. The cause isn't established yet.
  5. Farah: Will Nia see the earlier visit? I don't want to start again as though nobody has worked on the vehicle.
  6. Daniel: Yes. I'll connect this report to the service history so she can review it alongside the previous record.
  7. Farah: Does that mean you're accepting that the repair failed? I need to understand what your apology actually covers.
  8. Daniel: I'm correcting my response. The underlying cause is still unknown, so I can't say the earlier work failed or that this concern is unrelated.
  9. Farah: Fair enough. Who is actually going to follow this up, and when will I hear from them?
  10. Daniel: Nia, our service lead, can call at 15:00 to review your report and discuss the next arrangements.
  11. Farah: Three this afternoon, by phone? I don't need to bring the car in at three?
  12. Daniel: Right, it's a callback at three. It isn't a repair booking, a collection time, or a promise of completion.
  13. Farah: I'll be available. But I do need to ask about cost: the service was only two days ago.
  14. Daniel: I understand. Warranty coverage hasn't been determined. I can't promise free work or rule coverage out from what we have.
  15. Farah: Please pass that question on too. I don't want the call to end with me explaining the cost concern all over again.
  16. Daniel: I'll put it in the follow-up record with your report and my correction. There won't be a coverage decision recorded before one is made.
  17. Farah: Could you read back the arrangement, so I know exactly what Nia will receive?
  18. Daniel: Your repeat concern is linked to the earlier visit: same intermittent rattle, two days later. My dismissal is withdrawn; Nia can call at 15:00 to review it.
  19. Farah: Yes. I understand the call doesn't settle the cause or the cost, but at least the earlier report won't be lost.
  20. Daniel: That's the start of the communication repair. I'll pass on the complete history and coverage question, with the agreed call time and no invented outcome.
Dialogue in the book, page 73 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Correcting an overconfident denial

An advisor repairs an earlier response to a repeat concern.

  1. Advisor: I spoke too definitively when I said it couldn't be the same issue.
  2. Customer: I heard the rattle again two days after service. I need that taken seriously.
  3. Advisor: We'll review your report alongside the earlier repair order without assuming its cause.
  4. Customer: Are you promising the next visit is covered by warranty, or is that undecided?
  5. Advisor: Coverage is unconfirmed. Nia can call at three to discuss the report and next arrangements.
  6. Customer: Please don't treat my warranty inquiry as proof that the earlier work failed.
  7. Advisor: I won't. A recurring symptom, its cause, and applicable coverage need separate review.
  8. Customer: Can the callback use the same case reference so I don't repeat everything?
  9. Advisor: Yes. I'll link the previous visit and today's report for Nia's callback.
  10. Customer: Thank you. That corrects the earlier dismissal without making a new unsupported promise.
The same symptom may have a different cause

A service advisor takes a repeat-visit inquiry.

  1. Customer: The display went blank again. Does that prove the same component failed twice?
  2. Advisor: I can't establish that from the symptom alone. The technician needs to review the recurrence.
  3. Customer: Please connect this call with last month's repair order so the history is available.
  4. Advisor: I will. I'll record your new report with today's date and the earlier reference.
  5. Customer: I'd also like someone to explain whether any warranty terms apply to this concern.
  6. Advisor: I can refer that warranty inquiry, but I can't confirm coverage before review.
  7. Customer: Does asking the question automatically book another assessment or approve a diagnostic charge?
  8. Advisor: No. We need to discuss any proposed appointment, scope, and terms separately.
  9. Customer: Then for now I'm requesting a callback, not approving a new job.
  10. Advisor: I've recorded that accurately and will ask the service desk to confirm the callback arrangement.
A missed callback gets a concrete repair

A customer asks about a promised service response.

  1. Customer: I was promised a callback yesterday afternoon, but I didn't receive one.
  2. Advisor: I'm sorry we missed that contact commitment. Let me check the actual case status.
  3. Customer: Please don't tell me the technical review is complete unless someone has confirmed it.
  4. Advisor: The review remains pending. Our missed call and the unresolved assessment are separate issues.
  5. Customer: I need a named contact rather than another vague promise that someone will respond.
  6. Advisor: Service lead Omar has accepted the callback for four today using this case reference.
  7. Customer: Will that be a status update even if the technical review is still unfinished?
  8. Advisor: Yes. He will explain the current status, not guarantee a conclusion by four.
  9. Customer: Thank you. Please include that I prefer a call, because I may miss a text.
  10. Advisor: I've confirmed the phone preference and Omar's ownership of the four-o'clock callback.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Correct the dismissal before arranging the callback
  1. Complete the complaint-recovery dialogue.
  2. Read the apology and next-contact exchanges aloud. Name Nia, 15:00, and the purpose of the call; retain the unresolved cause and coverage.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Recover the conversation accurately

Complete the follow-up readback. Keep the apology, report, contact time, and unresolved coverage distinct.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Advisor: "I should not have ___ your report."

Answer and explanation

B. dismissed

Dismissed identifies the unsupported response that Daniel must explicitly correct.

2. Customer: "The rattle is still ___."

Answer and explanation

D. intermittent

Intermittent preserves the reported pattern instead of changing it to a constant sound.

3. Advisor: "Nia can call at ___."

Answer and explanation

A. 15:00

15:00 is the stated review-call time, not a repair completion time.

4. Customer: "Warranty coverage remains ___."

Answer and explanation

C. unknown

Unknown preserves the unresolved coverage position without promising or denying protection.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.
