Dialogue 07: The final bill differs from the estimate
Original fictional setting: An advisor and customer compare two amounts without guessing the reason.
Professional roles: Customer / Advisor
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Customer: The approved estimate totals three hundred, but this invoice says three twenty. Why?
Advisor: I can see the difference, but I shouldn't guess its reason. Let's compare the actual lines and approval record.
Customer: Please don't tell me it must be tax or a standard fee unless that is what this record shows.
Advisor: Agreed. A common explanation isn't evidence for your invoice. I'll ask the billing contact to identify the changed line.
Customer: I still want to understand the completed work, but the charge question needs a separate, accurate answer.
Advisor: I'll keep both questions visible: what the job record says was performed, and why the invoice amount differs.
Customer: Does requesting that explanation mean you will mark me as refusing all payment or disputing every listed item?
Advisor: No. I'll record your specific twenty-dollar query rather than broaden it beyond what you've actually asked.
Customer: Please compare the approved estimate's date and version, not an earlier proposal.
Advisor: I'll provide the date and reference alongside this invoice, leaving the reason for the difference unconfirmed until checked.
Customer: Arrange that explanation before anyone marks my specific charge query resolved.
Advisor: I will. The record will show a focused billing query awaiting clarification, not an invented justification or outcome.
Vocabulary in this exchange: estimate: A stated anticipated cost for a defined service scope, subject to its expressed terms.
END REFERENCE

Dialogue 08: A different concern after a previous visit
Original fictional setting: An advisor and customer clarify whether a new call concerns the same symptom.
Professional roles: Customer / Advisor
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Customer: I visited last week and now have another concern. Should they share a case?
Advisor: We can link the history, but could you describe what you've noticed so we don't assume the concerns are identical?
Customer: Last week's visit concerned a display setting. This time I hear a sound near the rear of the cabin.
Advisor: Thank you. I'll record a newly reported sound and link the earlier visit as history, without asserting a connection.
Customer: I'd like the service lead to review whether the two might be related, rather than reception deciding either way.
Advisor: I can relay that question. The relationship, cause, and any applicable coverage remain unconfirmed until the appropriate review.
Customer: Does linking the records automatically confirm warranty coverage or waive assessment charges?
Advisor: No. Any proposed appointment, scope, and terms need their own explanation; the reference link alone doesn't decide those matters.
Customer: Then please arrange a callback before any new job is authorized.
Advisor: I'll ask the service lead to confirm a callback time and use the linked references for context.
Customer: Keep the display concern and new sound separate in the handoff.
Advisor: I will. The handoff will show the prior display concern and the new sound report as distinct accounts.
END REFERENCE

Customer: Does linking the records automatically confirm warranty coverage or waive assessment charges?
Advisor: No. Any proposed appointment, scope, and terms need their own explanation; the reference link alone doesn't decide those matters.
Customer: Then please arrange a callback before any new job is authorized.
Advisor: I'll ask the service lead to confirm a callback time and use the linked references for context.
Customer: Keep the display concern and new sound separate in the handoff.
Advisor: I will. The handoff will show the prior display concern and the new sound report as distinct accounts.
END REFERENCE

4 ways to practice

Ready to paste: the complete prompt is already written for you, including the selected lesson or dialogue. Choose an activity and copy it; no prompt writing or assembly is required.

Try the lesson or rehearse the dialogue first. Then choose a goal and copy a carefully scripted prompt into your preferred AI. The prompt includes the actual case, language and professional context.

You take one role. The AI answers as the other professional.

These settings adjust the practice; they are not an assessment of your level.

Read your ready-to-use prompt 
Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions after the REFERENCE block. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Automotive Service English
Professional audience: Service reception staff, service advisors, automotive technicians, parts-counter staff, and workshop assistants
Scope: The vehicles, shop terms, charges, records, and events are fictional. This book teaches English, not vehicle diagnosis, repair, testing, driving decisions, or legal advice. Follow actual training, authorization, manufacturer information, shop procedures, and applicable requirements. A code, invoice, or customer description does not establish vehicle safety, readiness, repair necessity, or warranty coverage. The dialogues give no instructions to reproduce symptoms while driving or work on a vehicle.
Lesson 01: Capturing the customer's concern
Original fictional case: Two household cars are listed on the account, but today Mina has booked the blue hatchback. She reports an occasional rattle from the rear after the car has been parked overnight. She does not know the cause, and no technician assessment has occurred. Advisor Ellis must confirm the vehicle, preserve the reported sound and conditions, and read back the concern. Mina is describing existing observations; she is not asked to drive or perform a test to reproduce the sound.
Language workshop: The blue hatchback and an occasional rear rattle
Communication goal: Identify the booked vehicle and capture an intermittent customer concern without inventing its frequency, cause, or technical finding.
Language guidance: The booked vehicle is the blue hatchback, not both cars on the account. The sound is occasional and reported from the rear after overnight parking. Do not change those details to a constant noise or a named failed component.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Which of the two cars is booked today? / I have the blue hatchback for this appointment. / I will not attach this concern to the other vehicle. / How have you described the sound you noticed? / You report a rattle from the rear. / It is occasional, not constant. / You have noticed it after the car was parked overnight. / You have not identified what causes it. / No technician assessment has taken place yet. / I will record rattle without naming a failed component. / The customer reports the following concern. / This description does not establish whether the vehicle is fault-free. / A model name and a vehicle identification number are different. / Does that wording accurately describe your experience? / Blue hatchback; occasional rear rattle after overnight parking; cause unknown. / I will put that concern on the job card for assessment.
Vocabulary: service advisor: Staff member coordinating customer communication and workshop work. / customer concern: Issue described by the vehicle owner or user. / vehicle identification: Information establishing which vehicle a record concerns. / service interval: Specified time or accumulated-distance basis for scheduled maintenance, subject to the actual vehicle schedule. / hatchback: Car body style with an upward-opening rear door to the cargo area. / booking reference: Identifier for a scheduled service appointment. / tire aspect ratio: Nominal sidewall height expressed as a percentage of nominal section width in a tire-size designation. / vehicle identification number: Unique vehicle identifier commonly abbreviated VIN. / load index: Tire rating code associated with load capacity, not a literal weight in kilograms. / model: Named vehicle design within a manufacturer's range. / state of charge: Battery charge level expressed as a percentage on the applicable battery system's basis. / odometer: Instrument recording accumulated vehicle distance. / intermittent: Occurring at intervals rather than continuously. / rattle: Repeated light knocking or vibrating sound. / rear: Part or direction toward the back of the vehicle. / overnight parking: Period during which a vehicle remains parked through the night. / reported conditions: Circumstances described as accompanying the concern. / frequency: How often a symptom is reported to occur. / total toe: Combined toe value for the two wheels on an axle under the report's sign convention. / symptom description: Account of the experienced sound, behavior, or condition. / technician assessment: Relevant professional evaluation of the concern. / angular minute: One sixtieth of a degree, not one hundredth of a degree. / job card: Workshop record containing the requested work and relevant details. / concern readback: Repetition of the customer's account to confirm accuracy.
Speaking task: Read the concern in the brief, then complete the dialogue using the supplied word bank. Read Mina's turns aloud, stressing occasional, rear, and after overnight parking. Then read Ellis's matching readback. Complete the four-line transfer and check each answer against the explained key. Keep the vehicle and reported conditions unchanged.
Partner: Mina: Customer / Ellis: Service advisor
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the intake summary. Preserve the correct car, sound, location, and reported condition.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Mina: Yes, that readback matches. No fixed frequency, and no part identified.
Ellis: Here is the concern readback: customer reports an occasional rear rattle in the blue hatchback after overnight parking. Cause unknown; no technician assessment has occurred.
Mina: Thank you. That's what I wanted recorded, without making the sound seem constant.
Ellis: I will retain that customer concern for the workshop handoff. The next assessment can build on an accurate description instead of starting from an assumed cause.
END REFERENCE

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Paste into a new chat, then follow the exercise one step at a time. English Ladder does not send anything to an AI service. Your chosen service's terms and any usage charges apply.

AI responses can contain mistakes. Compare feedback with the lesson and check specialist claims against the course references. Use these exercises for language practice; follow qualified guidance for actual professional decisions.

For teachers and study partners

Ask learners to respond before revealing the model. Give feedback on one meaning issue and one language pattern, then let them repeat. For mixed levels, offer the frames first and remove them in the second round.

Use the learner book for extended dialogue practice, vocabulary and collocations, reusable phrases, and explained answer keys.
